Iconographia — The Images of Tycho Brahe

The images of Tycho Brahe, edited: every likeness made in his lifetime or from living memory, the programme of images he controlled, and the afterlife of the face — each record with its dating argument, its whereabouts and their evidence, its versions, its disagreements, its open questions, and a link to the object where one exists.

32 records · 18 in the lifetime series · 22 extant · 16 located under a live institutional record · 28 versions and exemplars · 18 derivations · 84 sources · 1581–1901, the Kronborg tapestry to the schoolroom sheet

Edition of 5 September 2026 · how to cite · data (JSON) · catalogue (CSV)

1Introduction

This is the catalogue of the images of Tycho Brahe. It holds three series. The lifetime series is every documented likeness made while he lived or from the memory of people who had seen him: the paintings on wood, wall and instrument pedestal, the woodcut, the two Amsterdam engravings and the drawing behind the second, the gilt binding die, the medal, the exile portrait, the watercolours in the finest Mechanica copies, and the Prague effigy that closes the series. The image programme is the wider set of images he controlled: the arms stamp that partners the portrait die, the engraved portrait inserted loose in his books, the emblem-quadrant, the printed views of his buildings, and his portrait on the celestial globes. The afterlife follows the face from the first posthumous plates to the nineteenth-century history painting and the children’s memorial sheet.

The baseline is Harald Mortensen’s census of the portraits (1946), read against F. R. Friis’s descriptions of the 1890s, the exhibition volume Tycho Brahes Verden (2006), the print catalogues (Westergaard, the New Hollstein, van Regteren Altena), and Tycho’s own letters and printed words about his likeness. Every record has then been checked against the object or its institutional digitisation wherever one is public, and the plates in this edition are taken from those digitisations. Mortensen’s census is treated as a finding-list of what he located, not as an authority on what the objects are: several of his verdicts are stated without a basis, and three of them are corrected here on the evidence of the objects themselves.

Each record states what the image is, who made it and when; on what evidence the dates rest; where the object is today and how firmly that is known; whether it is an original or a copy; which versions and exemplars are known and where; where the sources disagree; what has been settled; and what remains open. The apparatus is the point of the edition, and none of it is withheld.

The unit is the image, not the object. A record is one likeness or one image type. The second engraving is one record although impressions of it sit in a dozen print rooms; the Mechanica watercolours are one record although five leaves survive and each is unique; the portrait die is one record although its impressions are on bindings across Europe. Where a record has several physical carriers, they are listed under it as versions and exemplars, and again by holding institution in §4.

Three questions are kept apart because the literature routinely runs them together. Does the thing survive? is the record’s status: extant, lost, untraced (last seen at a stated date, not found since) or attested (reported by one witness, never confirmed). Where is it? is the whereabouts tier, which states how firmly the present location is held: located means a live record-level catalogue entry or an institution naming the object; attested means somebody once said so, sometimes a century ago; for an edition multiple it says what survives and where the reproduced impression is. Is it the original? is the record’s nature, answered separately: for one whole type the original is gone and every located painting is a copy of a lost picture rather than of any surviving one.

Dates are bounds with arguments. A record that can be dated carries a lower and an upper bound, each fixed by a stated argument — a dated letter, a dedication, an inscribed age, a painter’s death, an object that had to exist for something documented to happen. The bounds are printed with their arguments in §5. One convention recurs: in this family the inscribed ‘aetatis 40 … 1586’ names the origin of the likeness and travels with the design; it never dates the object that carries it.

Record keys. Every record prints its key (icon-degheyn-v2) and is addressable: #icon-degheyn-v2 as an anchor, or ?record=icon-degheyn-v2, which highlights it. Sources are cited by short form; every short form links to its full entry in §9.

The figures, and what they mean

FigureCountDefinition
Records32one per likeness or image type, never per object; the three series partition them
… the lifetime series18likenesses made in his lifetime or from living memory — including the Týn effigy (after 1604), the positional funeral plate, and the conjectural tapestry figure (see the limits below)
… the image programme5images he controlled that are not likenesses of his face, or not primarily so
… the afterlife9posthumous derivatives, from the 1601 copy-plate to the children’s sheet
Extant / lost / untraced / attested22 / 5 / 4 / 1the status axis: does the thing survive (an edition multiple counts as extant when impressions survive)
Located16records whose principal object is held under a live record-level catalogue entry or an institution naming it; the rest are attested, untraced, destroyed, or edition multiples
Illustrated22records with a plate (21 distinct plates: the inserted book portrait shares the second engraving’s)
Versions and exemplars28physical carriers enumerated under the records that have several
Dated bounds26records carrying an argued lower and upper bound (§5)
Derivations18edges in the descent of the likeness (§6), each with its kind and basis
Disagreements38recorded conflicts between the sources, each with a status
Questions76 + 9open questions on the records, plus the catalogue’s own standing questions; 2 settled and kept as settled
Rejected identifications4images called portraits of Tycho and refused, with the ground (§7)
Links to objects19 + 38records whose principal object links to the holding institution’s own record, plus sources with a verified open-access or record link
Sources84every work or record cited, with what it contributes (§9)

What the figures are not. The eighteen records of the lifetime series are a count of records, not of likenesses taken from life. Two of the eighteen stand in the series on other grounds and the edition says so in each: the Týn effigy was erected after 1604, from the memory of people who had seen him, and the funeral plate of 1589 is a positional claim — Tycho among the canopy-bearers — not a likeness at all. A third, the Kronborg tapestry figure, is there on a conjectural identification that has never been established. The count of extant records is not a count of surviving objects: edition multiples survive in numbers nobody has counted, and the versions tables list only the exemplars actually located. ‘Illustrated’ counts records with a plate; two records share one plate, since the inserted book portrait is the second engraving. No grade is a statement about survival, and no status is a statement about location.

The evidence grades

GradeNameAs applied in this editionRecords
ASecurethe identification and the dating rest on the object itself or on a contemporary document — Tycho's own letters or printed words, an inscription read on the object, a signed plate14
BProbablethey rest on a reliable later witness or on well-supported inference — an attribution by consensus without a signature, a copy of a lost original, a date fixed by its carrier11
CPossiblethey rest on conjecture or on a single uncorroborated witness — a resemblance in a background figure, a report never confirmed, a credit line, an undefended judgement7
DDoubtfulnot applied: nothing in this edition is graded D. An image whose connection to Tycho is that weak is listed under the rejected identifications instead—

3Register of the images

The same thirty-two records in full, in the order of the three series and chronologically within each. Each record carries its plate at reading size where a public reproduction exists and says in words where none does; its maker and date; the description; the original-or-copy verdict; the dated bounds with their arguments; the whereabouts with their evidence and the holding institution’s own record; the versions and exemplars known; the copy-level carriers where the image lives inside books; the disagreements between the sources, with their status; the questions settled and the questions open; the relations to other records and to the other pillars; and the sources.

The lifetime series

18 records
1

The portraits on the equatorial armillary's pedestal

lifetimelostAicon-armillary-pedestal-portraits
c. 1584 (instrument built) – December 1596 (the recording block's first issue); the inscribed '1580' is Tycho's floruit year, not an execution date
Four portraits were painted on the wooden pedestal of the smaller equatorial armillary — the instrument of the Mechanica's Fig. 12, whose pedestal Tycho describes as built 'e solido et forti ligno'; not the great steel-based armillary of Fig. 14, built later, whose chapter mentions no portraits. Tycho and Copernicus occupied the two visible niches, Ptolemy and Albategnius the hidden reverse. Tycho names them himself in the Mechanica: 'sunt 4. Icones … quae effigies Ptolemæi, Albategnij et Copernici; tum quoque NOSTRAM repræsentant' — and ours too. The instrument put him into the lineage physically, on the object that did the measuring. Blaeu re-engraved the whole plate on copper for the Grooten Atlas (Amsterdam 1664, Hven section): the pedestal programme is carried over intact — both niches with their figures, the plumb-bob cartouche, and the year-tablets, with '1520' engraved in full (the woodcut's carved bracket no longer obscures the leading digit) — while the right entablature is re-lettered 'T.B.N.D.' against the woodcut's 'T-BR-D' (engraver's error or a deliberate re-reading; which, is undetermined). Derivative of the woodcut rather than of the lost instrument, but the fullest posthumous transmission of the portrait programme. The instrument's place is settled from Tycho's own Uraniborg room-list: 'S. Observatorium minus Septentrionale, alias Armillas AEquatorias continens num. 12' — the lesser north observatory of the main house, with the steel sibling (Fig. 13) in the south room under key N; the house-key letters invert the compass sense, which also explains Blaeu's 'Armillae S' section heading. The same room assignment already stands in the 1596 Epistolae. The entablature initials follow the lineage formula of the sibling Stjerneborg headings (name + origin): T-BR-D = Tycho BRahe Danus (anchored by Chytraeus's 'Tycho Brahe Danus de Knudstryp'), N-C-T-G = Nicolaus Copernicus Torunensis G[ermanus] - the G a structural inference completing the Danus/Germanus pairing, nowhere spelled out. The tablets' years are round-decade floruit values like the whole Stjerneborg series (300 BC...1520, 1580); 1520 = Copernicus's Frombork observing maturity in Tycho's assignment; what 1580 commemorates stays open (Uraniborg's completion leads, per the Mechanica's own 'CIRCA ANNUM 1580' caption). Against Blaeu's re-lettered 'T.B.N.D.', the woodcut's T-BR-D governs: the lifetime block was cut under Tycho's control, and its crude period R invites exactly the misreading the 1664 engraver made. The block's first issue was not the Mechanica: the Icones instrumentorum suite (Uraniborg 1596, Nielsen 975 — bare image-pulls without letterpress, keyed to the Epistolae's instrument descriptions) already carries it, so the portrait programme is attested by December 1596. Four Icones copies are documented (Stockholm KB, Vienna, Wroclaw, and one Epistolae-and-Icones volume in the trade).
Maker
Unrecorded. Christianson's 'probably painted by Gemperle' remains an uncited hedge (grade C) — Beckett & Christensen 1921 never mention these portraits, and DBL's Gemperlin entry is silent on the armillary. Gemperlin WAS available: recruited at Augsburg 1575, royal settlement letter 23 April 1577, documented in Denmark from 1578, died 1587 of a Copenhagen epidemic on Tycho's own printed testimony (De mundi II, p. 284) — his residency brackets the whole preferred painting window.
Original or copy
The painted originals are destroyed with the instruments. What survives is the record: the Mechanica plate.
Dated c. 1584 → December 1596 (the Icones instrumentorum issue)
Name-labels N·C·T·G / T·BR·D sit on the entablatures above the niches; the year-tablets on the plinths beneath the figures read '…520' (leading digit obscured by the block's own carved bracket in every impression examined) and '1580' ('I580' is the font's flag-serif numeral 1). The years are floruit labels, settled by direct inspection of Nathan Chytraeus, Variorum in Europa itinerum deliciae (Herborn 1594), pp. 596–598 (inspected August 2026): the Stjerneborg hypocaustum cycle — Tycho's own sibling lineage programme, on material his preface says came largely from Tycho himself — gives every figure an 'Anno X' floruit line, with COPERNICUS 'Anno 1520 post Christum' and TYCHO BRAHE 'Anno 1580': the identical pair of years the pedestal tablets carry. The pedestal therefore uses the same convention (and the obscured '…520' is confirmed as 1520); '1580' cannot in any case date the execution, since no armillary existed then. Christianson 2000's reading of 1580 as a construction year (against his own 2020 chronology) is thereby retired; what 1580 commemorates within Tycho's career (Uraniborg's completion is Dreyer's candidate) remains open, and Zeeberg 1995 ('The Inscriptions at Tycho Brahe's Uraniborg', in Skafte Jensen ed., Odense 1995, pp. 251–66 — undigitised; no scan located as of August 2026) is now corroborative rather than load-bearing. If Christianson's hedged Gemperlin attribution were adopted, the upper bound would fall to 1587; it stays 1598 (publication of the recording plate). The 1585 Icones instrumentorum (Nielsen 976, Uraniborg broadsides, Marburg unicum — enclosed with Tycho's 1 March 1586 letter to Wilhelm IV) was checked as a possible earlier attestation (August 2026): its armillary woodcut depicts the steel armilla (Fig. 13 type; 'pes...ex solido metallo'), not this instrument — a diagnostic null that leaves the 1598 plate the earliest witness to the portraits. The hard upper bound is December 1596, not 1598: the Icones instrumentorum suite (Nielsen 975, Uraniborg 1596, 18 letterpress-free woodcut pulls issued with some Epistolae copies; presentation copies to Christian IV and Chancellor Friis in December 1596) contains the armillary printed from what is almost certainly the same woodblock (Stockholm KB exemplar examined leaf-by-leaf: identical tablets, V-medallion, and the full compositor letter-key in identical positions; print-flaw-level confirmation pending an uncoloured 1598 impression). The painting window is therefore summer 1584 to December 1596, preferred sub-window 1584–87 unchanged. The block itself was cut somewhere between 1584 and 1596; the documented production waves (two blocks 1585, two 1586–87 for De mundi, several 1592–93 for the Progymnasmata, boxwood-only from April 1590) make the post-1590 campaign the likeliest home for it, stated as preference.
Whereabouts

Painted originals destroyed with Tycho's instruments at Prague. The plate survives in every intact copy of the 1598 Mechanica.

HolderDet Kongelige Bibliotek (hand-coloured exemplar), Copenhagen
InventoryLN 432 2°
Evidencedestroyed destroyed (originals); located (the recording plate)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

The Mechanica's instrument plates are woodcuts, not engravings — only six illustrations in the book are from copperplates. The pedestal plate is printed from the identical woodblock in the 1602 Hulsius reissue — the inscriptions are letter-for-letter identical; the one armillary re-engraved on copper in 1602 is Fig. 13, not this plate. Fate: not among the four giant instruments left on Hven in 1597 (Tycho's own Wandsbek list, TBOO XIII.101-104), so it travelled with the general train, reaching Bohemia only in October or November 1600 (Dreyer p. 320; Thoren JHA 1973 via Wolfschmidt). After 1601 the accounts fork — most instruments confiscated toward Vienna by 1604 (Schuppener after Kleinschnitzova) vs. destroyed after the 1619 Bohemian war (Wolfschmidt) — unreconciled; no fragment survives (only the two NTM sextants survive of all the instruments). Bergeron's 1603 Belvedere ensemble (four rulers + four astronomers, matching Resen 1668) is a different programme from this pedestal's two-name scheme.

Elsewhere in Tychoniana Mechanica (1598) · Northern equatorial armillary sphere

2

The Kronborg tapestry figure — the conjectured earliest likeness

lifetimeextantCicon-tapestry-figure
1581–1584 (woven)
In the great tapestry of Frederik II and the young Crown Prince Christian (IV), two small noblemen stand at a balustrade overlooking the Sound. The right-hand figure, facing the viewer, has since Wanscher (1939) been conjectured to be Tycho. Direct examination of the tapestry photograph at full resolution (July 2026) settled two points the literature left vague: the figure wears a soft beret with a jewelled band and a large white ostrich plume — a worn plumed hat — and around his neck three twisted chain strands, one carrying a round medallion on a link chain; no Order of the Elephant. That chain-and-medallion scheme matches both the young Christian IV and the candidate's own companion (identified in the literature as Gert Rantzau, lensmand of Kronborg) in the same weave — three figures carrying the same ornament, weighing toward workshop convention rather than personal insignia. It is tempting to date the figure by the absent Elephant, but the year Tycho received that order is nowhere documented and the usual 'c.1585/86' is itself inferred from portraits — so the costume corroborates the weaving date here rather than independently fixing anything. His companion leans beside an undetermined round object on the ledge — at full resolution it shows no meridian ring, banding, landmass, or stand, so it cannot be read as a globe rather than a hat, cushion, or plain ball. Taken together: if Wanscher's identification is right, a plumed-hatted Tycho image existed already in 1581–84, and the beret worn in the second de Gheyn engraving a decade later was a return to how he actually dressed, not an invention. That reading inherits the identification's conditional grade and cannot be pressed harder than it: the resemblance that suggests the identification cannot simultaneously prove the costume history.
Maker
Hans Knieper's tapestry workshop, Helsingør (the royal genealogy series for Kronborg)
Original or copy
An original weaving of 1581–84 — its originality is not in question. What is conjectural is the identification of the small right-hand nobleman as Tycho, first proposed by Wanscher (1939; Mortensen's '1930' a probable citation error) on facial resemblance in a background figure, and never established.
Dated 1581 → 1584
The dating is the one certainty: the royal genealogy tapestry series for Kronborg was woven 1581–1584, so the figure — whoever he is — falls in that window on hard documentary grounds. Everything else is conditional: the identification as Tycho, first proposed by Wanscher (Kronborgs Historie, 1939; Mortensen's 'Kbh. 1930' is a probable citation error — Wanscher's 1930 book is Rosenborgs Historie), rests on facial resemblance in a small background figure and has never been proven ('possibly correct' was Mortensen's verdict). If the identification holds, this is the earliest surviving likeness of Tycho, made when he was 35–38.
Whereabouts

On display at the National Museum in Copenhagen.

HolderNationalmuseet, Danmarks Middelalder og Renæssance, Copenhagen
InventoryGenstandsnummer CLXXIII (piece-level number for this tapestry)
Evidencelocated located (live record-level page on the museum's own database)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

Of the 15 survivors, Kronborg holds 7 king-tapestries; Nationalmuseet holds 7 king-tapestries plus 1 hunting tapestry from the same suite (per the 1718 Rosenborg inventory). Woven at Helsingør 1581–84 by Netherlandish weavers after designs by Hans Knieper; h. c. 390 × b. c. 365 cm. Beware the record's 'Datering 1 Jan 1970 – 21 July 2014' field: that is photo-file metadata, not the object's date.

Where the sources disagree 2
QuestionPositionsStatus
Is the figure Tycho at all?The 2006 book says 'må være Tycho Brahe' and Nationalmuseet's record says 'det må være Tycho Brache' — near-identical wording, i.e. ONE assertion from the owning institution, not two independent sources. Mortensen 1946 grades Wanscher's proposal only 'possibly right'.unresolved; grade C stands; the 2006 book's authority does not raise it
Does the figure wear the Order of the Elephant?Jern 1976 (p. 139, printed p. 142, n. 53) states the figure wears the Order of the Elephant ('behängd med elefantordén'). Wanscher's own text, quoted verbatim by Mortensen 1946, states the opposite: the figure still wears the golden chain with Frederik II's contrafej, the Elephant coming only in 1585 or 1586 — the Elephant's absence is in fact Wanscher's dating device. Direct image examination at full resolution confirms a round rosette-bordered medallion with no elephant form.resolved against Jern (a paraphrase error; Jern 1976 is no witness to the figure's costume); residual: Wanscher 1939 pp. 64–65 remain unread at source
Relations
  • depicts Tycho Brahe
Sources
Mortensen, 'Portræter af Tycho Brahe' (Cassiopeia, 1946) — the portrait censusJern, Uraniborg — herresäte och himlaborg (Lund 1976)Reindel, 'Bordhimlens dydsmønstre' (Renæssanceforum 2, 2006)The editor's research notes on the portrait series (July 2026)
3

The Steensgaard panel — a lifetime portrait or an early copy, never tested

lifetimeuntracedCicon-steensgaard-oil
the likeness c. mid-1580s; the panel untested
A portrait of Tycho at Hvedholm and later Steensgaard on Funen, which Mortensen thought probably the oldest picture of him preserved in Denmark — while calling it 'en ældre Kopi', an older copy, and 'ikke godt malet', not well painted. Whether it is a lifetime portrait or a later copy has never actually been tested, and this record deliberately leaves it open. The copy reading is the standing position, but its evidential chain is one link long: Mortensen gives no basis for it; he had published the opposite in 1937, listing the same picture as the only 'Originalmaleri (?)' of Tycho preserved in Denmark; and two paragraphs after calling it a copy in 1946 he writes that it was 'painted in the mid-1580s'. The only earlier verdict, Friis's, is explicitly presumptive — he judged a different picture a copy and added that the same 'presumably' applied to this one, recording no examination. Against that, several things point toward a genuine picture of its period. Hvedholm passed in 1611 to Tycho's own brother Steen Brahe and stayed in Brahe hands until 1786, and the house held other dated sixteenth-century family portraits. The clumsiness is itself characteristic of the Danish and Scanian portraits of the 1580s. The composition matches no known print of Tycho. And most tellingly, the astronomical attributes — dividers, an armillary sphere with its meridian and great circles, a cross-staff — are restoration overpaint: strip them and nothing astronomical remains, only a plain Danish noble portrait in the standard formula of the decade. Someone assembling a 'Tycho Brahe' from the prints would have painted an astronomer; here a later hand had to add one. One physical detail appears in no publication: a 'T B' monogram in Roman capitals at the upper left, above the Brahe arms. Initials beside arms is a real sixteenth-century convention and equally what a later labeller would add, so it settles nothing — but it has been missed. Uniquely in his census, Mortensen records no dimensions, no support and no medium for this picture, so even 'oil on panel' is convention rather than attestation. The panel has not been traced since the Copenhagen auction that dispersed the Steensgaard contents (V. Winkel & Magnussen, 20 October 1938); no second, Hvedholm sale has been traced — the Hvedholm main building had passed to the state in 1928 and the contents most plausibly moved to Steensgaard with Hendrik Bille-Brahe-Selby. Mortensen's 1946 'at Steensgaard' repeats a location he had already published in 1937. The question is closable: every securely dated comparandum in this Danish group is oil on oak, so dendrochronology of the support would settle lifetime-versus-later outright.
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
Open, and never tested. Mortensen called it 'en ældre Kopi' (an older copy) and 'ikke godt malet', yet in 1937 published the same picture as the only 'Originalmaleri (?)' of Tycho preserved in Denmark, and elsewhere dated it to the mid-1580s. No source records the panel's support, medium or dimensions, so no physical datum exists against which any candidate could be matched.
Dated c. 1584 → c. 1588
These bounds date the likeness the panel carries, not the panel itself, whose status is genuinely open. The costume places the likeness in the mid-1580s: Tycho wears the Frederik II medal and not the Order of the Elephant. That argument is weaker than it looks and should not be leaned on hard — the year Tycho received the Elephant is nowhere documented (Friis: he must have had it from Frederik II, 'but when, or on what occasion, is not known'), and the usual 'c.1585/86' is itself inferred from portraits, so dating portraits by it is circular. What the panel plainly is not is a picture of the later Tycho: it shows neither the Elephant of the 1586–87 oil nor the stacked chains of the engravings.
Whereabouts

Untraced since 1938 — but a strong candidate has now surfaced.

HolderCandidate: Det Nationalhistoriske Museum på Frederiksborg, Hillerød, Denmark
InventoryA 8191 (candidate identification, not confirmed)
Evidenceuntraced untraced for the panel Mortensen photographed; the candidate A 8191 is itself located
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

The panel disappears after the 1938 Copenhagen dispersal of the Steensgaard contents following Hendrik lensgreve Bille-Brahe-Selby's death on 7 May 1938 (Danmarks Adels Aarbog 1985-87:561-23c, via finnholbek.dk I11378; danmarkshistorien.lex.dk gives 1937, recorded as a minority reading); the dispersal auction followed on 20 October 1938. Frederiksborg A 8191 — 'Ubekendt', dated ca. 1600, hanging in Stue 27, sitter recorded as Brahe, Tyge Ottesen — is a painting of exactly this type, and is the picture reproduced uncredited on the dust jacket of Tycho Brahes Verden (2006), whose source photograph Nationalmuseet credits to Frederiksborg.

Where the sources disagree 2
QuestionPositionsStatus
Is Frederiksborg A 8191 the Steensgaard panel?FOR: the composition matches Mortensen's 1946 plate in every element — the T B monogram, the crest at upper left, the curtain ground, the meshed shoulder chain with a separate beaded centre chain, the Frederik II medallion, the armillary sphere and cross-staff at lower left, the object in the left hand. Frederiksborg's 'ca. 1600' is compatible with Mortensen's 'oldest picture of him preserved in Denmark'. Frederiksborg is the buyer one would expect at a 1938 Copenhagen dispersal of Danish historical portraits. Against: the gap between inscription and crest, and the crest's size relative to the inscription, do not match the 1946 plate — a mismatch re-corroborated by direct re-inspection of the scale-matched composite built for the July 2026 examination, and not dismissible as halftone noise (the foreshortening escape was tested and rejected). Two further findings cut against the identification without settling it: the moustache form of the Commons photograph groups with the original drooping (non-Hvidt) type — evidence against A 8191 being the 1937-photographed Hvidt copy, but non-diagnostic between 'is the Steensgaard panel' and 'is a further version'; and Karlson 1928 (note 24) attests a 1923 Hvedholm-to-Frederiksborg portrait transfer with copies substituted in place, keeping a 1923 arrival at Frederiksborg live as an alternative route.unresolved; the measurement against was re-corroborated on the scale-matched composite and is not dismissible as halftone noise, but it remains a photographic reading of a dirty, abraded picture and is not decisive either way
Lifetime portrait or later copy?Mortensen 1946 says 'ældre Kopi'; Mortensen 1937 published it as the only 'Originalmaleri (?)' in Denmark; Friis's copy verdict is explicitly presumptive ('formentlig') and extended by analogy from a different picture.open; closable only by examining the object — dendrochronology has never been attempted
Open questions 5
  1. The accession date and provenance of A 8191, which only the museum's own registry can supply: an acquisition in or after 1938 from the Bille-Brahe-Selby dispersal would close the identification. The national Kunstindeks Danmark is no substitute — Frederiksborg is absent from its roster of museums, and a museum filter there returns no artwork records.
  2. Harald Mortensen, 'Den ældste Tycho Brahe-portræt', Nordisk astronomisk tidsskrift n.s. xvi (1935), p. 58 — not yet consulted. The title matches his superlative for this panel exactly, and it predates both his 1937 'Originalmaleri (?)' and his 1946 'ældre Kopi'. No digitisation has been found (ADS, Runeberg, tidsskrift.dk and the Internet Archive are all negative; the 1934 Norwegian companion piece, read at nb.no, is silent on Steensgaard), so the article can only be read in the Royal Library.
  3. The 1938 dispersal catalogue, Fortegnelse over indbo fra Steensgaard (V. Winkel & Magnussen, auction 20 October 1938): a lot entry would give dimensions, support, attribution and the buyer. Copies are in the trade and in the Royal Library's auction-catalogue files. One caution: a trade listing transcribes the title-page decedent as 'Baron Preben Bille-Brahe-Selby' against the documented Hendrik lensgreve — most likely a transcription error, but not settled until the title page is seen.
  4. Daniel Hvidt's two painted copies — M/S Museet for Søfart, Helsingør, and the Astronomisk-Historisk Samling at Rundetårn — rest on Mortensen 1946 alone and have not been confirmed with the institutions.
  5. Should the panel be located, dendrochronology, then infrared reflectography and X-radiography, would settle lifetime against later and whether the astronomical attributes are restoration overpaint, as Mortensen suggested.

4

The Stjerneborg astronomer cycle — Tycho as the terminus of his own science

lifetimelostAicon-stjerneborg-astronomers
1584 – 1594
Painted above the carved cornice of the warming room at Stjerneborg: eight astronomers reclining in succession — Timochares, Hipparchus, Ptolemy, Albategnius, Alfonso, Copernicus, then TYCHO himself holding a painted slip lettered QVID SI SIC? ('what if thus?'), and finally an eighth figure named TYCHONIDES, a descendant yet to come. The dome above carried his own world system. It is the most explicit surviving act of Tycho's pictorial self-fashioning: he paints himself as the terminus of the entire history of astronomy and then adds an heir after himself. Resen 1668 (pp. 344–345) additionally records, near the centre of the room in a separate space, a distinct vanitas composition — a laurel-crowned philosophising youth with celestial globe and book, opposed to Death guarding worldly treasure, under VIVIMUS INGENIO, CAETERA MORTIS ERUNT — not to be conflated with the Tychonides figure.
Maker
Unrecorded. Tycho names nobody. Beckett & Christensen assume Tobias Gemperlin 'stiltiende' and attach the meteorological-diary painter entries of spring 1591 to this cycle — impossible, since Gemperlin was dead by 1587. No source documents any painter. Hans van Steenwinckel — documented at Stjerneborg's design and construction (c. 1584, resident on Hven through 1590) and as painter of the mural quadrant's perspectival architecture — is the best-profiled alternative, likewise unattested for this cycle. Christianson's Gemperle sentence restates Beckett (his entry cites Christensen & Beckett 1921, 18–21) and is not independent evidence.
Original or copy
Original wall painting, DESTROYED. No painted-plaster fragment is attested in the excavation literature (1823–24, 1901 — explicit published negative, 1902, 1924, 1951) or in the K-samsök federated heritage database (queried July 2026); SHM's and DigitaltMuseum's own databases and the Tycho Brahe Museum inventory remain unchecked. The 1946 Lund jubilee 'Stjerneborg motto' wall decoration is a modern recreation, not a survival.
Dated 1584 → 1594
Stjerneborg was built in 1584, so nothing could be painted in its warming room before that. The completed eight-figure cycle, including the Tychonides panel, is described as painted — icons with gold-lettered distichs in the hypocaustum — in Nathan Chytraeus, Variorum in Europa itinerum deliciae (Herborn 1594), pp. 596–598, on material Chytraeus's preface says came largely from Tycho himself; Tycho's own fuller printed description follows in the Epistolae astronomicae (1596). The painting campaign in the meteorological diary falls in spring 1591, inside the window but tied to this cycle by no source.
Whereabouts

Destroyed. The superstructure was demolished or decayed in the earlier 17th century; Charlier excavated the foundations in 1901.

PlaceSite only: Stjerneborg, island of Ven, Skåne, Sweden
Evidencedestroyed destroyed
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

The excavated crypts are open to visitors, but nothing of the painting survives.

Where the sources disagree 1
QuestionPositionsStatus
Who is 'Tychonides'?'A still unborn … possible descendant' — Beckett & Christensen 1921. 'Tænkte arvtager' (notional heir), »Tychonides« in scare quotes — Zeeberg 1993, pp. 18–21. 'The Tychonides, Tycho's male offspring' (literal but unspecific) — Mosley 2007, p. 25. 'Dvs. Tychos søn' — Grinder-Hansen ed. 2006, flat exhibition caption, unargued. Textual anchors: the authorial 1596 print is third-person and nameless, and TYCHONIDES alone among the eight headings carries no floruit line (Resen 1668 p. 343, page-image verified); the earlier Chytraeus 1594 state is first-person ('ipse & inibo vias').unresolved; a patronymic figure read variously as the actual son or a generic heir

5

The Frederiksborg oil (attributed to Gemperlin) — known through C. A. Jensen's 1838 copy

lifetimelostBicon-gemperlin-oil-portrait
c. 1586–87 (original); burned 1859
The best-regarded painted likeness of Tycho: a lost life-size half-length oil showing him bare-headed and near-frontal, the Order of the Elephant on a double gold chain, both hands on his chest, four rings (including his signet) on the left hand, and the Brahe arms in the upper corner. The artificial nose-bridge — Beckett noted it 'evidently slightly reddened' — is shown more clearly than in any other portrait. Mortensen (1946, p. 60) describes the false nose and a scar running from the right eye up over the forehead as two distinct, coexisting features, citing Sandblad (Lychnos, 1937) for the scar as a duel memento; Sandblad itself remains unread, and the scar is not resolvable in the available reproduction chain. How the painting entered the Karen Brahe / Odense Adelige Jomfrukloster collection has two candidate routes, neither archivally proven: direct Brahe-family descent (the route Schepelern and Bruun Hansen treat as more probable) or transmission via Tycho's kinsman Holger Rosenkrantz (Schepelern's secondary 'måske', resting on a Sophie Brahe correspondence, and asserted flatly by Christianson (2000, p. 120) without a footnote of its own); the one primary document, Tycho's letter of 10 July 1597, is read by Mortensen (1946, p. 23) as concerning the full-length exile-type portrait, not this panel — and Gemperlin was dead by 1587 in any case. The picture was transferred by royal order of 9 October 1838 to the portrait gallery at Frederiksborg, and burned there in the great fire of 16–17 December 1859. It survives through C. A. Jensen's 1838 oil copy (84.5 × 68 cm, now Roskilde Kloster — the image shown here) and H. V. Bissen's 1858 pencil drawing (Ny Carlsberg Glyptotek). How faithful is the copy? The July 2026 adjudication: highly faithful in iconographic content — Jensen preserved the unflattering specifics (the reddened prosthesis, the four rings) an ennobling copyist would suppress, Bissen's independent drawing agrees on the whole scheme, and Høyen, who saw the original at Odense in 1830 (his Notitsbog, per Beckett 1897; Bruun Hansen 2013, citing Schepelern pp. 37, 56), himself called it 'nobler in form' and more youthful than the prints — so the nobility was in the original, not added by Jensen. Only the surface handling is genuinely unknowable: no witness records the lost painting's brushwork, and Jensen's smooth Golden-Age finish is plausibly his own.
Maker
Attributed to Tobias Gemperlin (Augsburg painter in Tycho's service from 1575, dead of the Copenhagen plague by 1587). The attribution is scholarly consensus resting on the working relationship, not on a signature or document — and the hedge is foundational, not late: Friis ('vel det sandsynligste, at det har været et Originalportræt, malet af Gemperlin') and Madsen ('vel') both hedged it at the outset, and Mortensen and the Dansk Biografisk Leksikon hedge it still. Beckett's 1897 case compared Jensen's copy only to the 1586 engraving and a sketch then under Goltzius's name — derivatives to derivatives, never to the signed Vedel 1578 or St Ibb 1578 anchors — and Karlson (1928), who also showed that Beckett attributed Gemperlin works from reproductions, doubted the attribution in a single unreasoned clause judged 'efter kopian'. Hans Knieper is excluded as a rival hand by Tycho's own division-of-labour statement on the 1587 mural quadrant.
Original or copy
Original destroyed. The life-size half-length oil burned with Frederiksborg Castle on the night of 16–17 December 1859. It survives only through C. A. Jensen's exact copy of 1838, made for the Odense convent as a replacement when the original left for Frederiksborg.
Dated late 1586 → 1587
The lower bound rests on Gassendi's report (1655 ed., p. 137) that toward the end of 1586 Tycho 'wished to be depicted' by Gemperlin — voluit, a volitional construction, not a flat report of a completed sitting; the 'fortieth birthday' precision sometimes attached to this is not in Gassendi's narrative but belongs to the caption of the volume's unrelated frontispiece print. The burned picture's own inscription, as reported by Friis (Bidrag til dansk Kunsthistorie, p. 229), 'Anno Domini 1587 aetatis suae 40', independently corroborates the 1586/87 window; a sweep of the TBOO correspondence for 1586–88 is negative. The Order of the Elephant the sitter wears supplies only a floor: Christianson (2020, p. 97) dates Tycho's grant to 3 May 1580 at Odense (quote verified; his footnote's archival source not yet traced), so the device gives a hard 1580 terminus post quem rather than a mid-1580s one. The upper bound is firmer: the painter's death. Tycho's own De mundi aetherei II (printed at Uraniborg 1587) records Gemperlin dead of the plague — so if the attribution holds, the panel cannot postdate 1587.
Whereabouts

The original no longer exists. The 1838 copy hangs at Roskilde Kloster, in Karen Brahes stue.

HolderRoskilde Kloster (Den Skeel-Juel-Brahe'ske Stiftelse), Roskilde, Denmark
Evidencelocated located — a dedicated 2013 study names the room it hangs in; no public inventory number
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

Gorm Bruun Hansen, 'Da Tycho Brahe gik i kloster — eller et billedes historie', Historisk Årbog for Roskilde Amt (2013), pp. 27-32, opens: 'På Roskilde Kloster hænger i Karen Brahes stue et enestående portræt af astronomen Tycho Brahe.' His caption gives the copy as 86 x 69 cm.

The woodcut 'Effigies' (Westergaard 1376) — the praedelineatum's record

lifetimeextantBicon-woodcut-1586
design 1586; in use by February 1592
The woodcut portrait of Tycho — four carriers reconciled (Nielsen's three plus the Copenhagen prospectus quaternion), of which two impressions are verified by direct examination (Copenhagen, Marburg); Berlin's carrier is extant, and the Königsberg copy was reported destroyed (Norlind 1970) — the starting point of the portrait campaign and the best record of the plain scheme Tycho himself wanted: a single modest chain, the beret lying on the parapet — the 'praedelineatum' against which he later judged de Gheyn's first engraving overloaded. Bareheaded, in an emblematic composition of aristocratic splendour; the suggestion that the composition was sketched by Hans van Stenwinkel the Elder rests on an uncited sentence in Christianson 2000 (p. 291). The heraldic error in the ancestral quarterings is object-verified on the Marburg impression — BELKER with a barry charge standing where Vasa belonged, and the spelling HONNER for the Rønnow quartering; it persisted, part-corrected, into the first engraving, and the full correction (STORMVASE) is documented on the second engraving only — the woodcut has no corrected state.
Maker
Anonymous cutter — Nielsen 1921 (p. 185) infers, from a 1585 letter and undeciphered monograms, that German wood-cutters resided at Uraniborg; no cutter is named for any Uraniborg block, and off-island cutting cannot be excluded (Tycho's wood-cutter was away in Germany, August 1585). After a model in the Gemperlin likeness family; that Gemperlin ever cut a block is explicitly not established. The Stenwinkel sketch attribution rests solely on an uncited sentence in Christianson 2000, p. 291.
Original or copy
An original block, printed. Four carriers reconciled, two impressions directly verified as the woodcut; the block itself does not survive.
Dated late 1586 → February 1592
The lower bound is the inscribed design date — 'aetatis 40 … 1586' names the sitting/design year, tied to Tycho's 40th birthday in December 1586. The upper bound is documentary: the block was in use on the title verso of the Progymnasmata prospectus distributed with letters of 20 February – 22 March 1592. The physical cutting date is open anywhere between those bounds. The 1588–90 wood-cutter recruitment letters concern instrument figures, never the portrait, and cannot anchor the block; the 25 August 1585 Hagecius letter shows cutting capacity intermittent and partly off-island. Both de Gheyn engravings retain the 1586 tablet years later — in this family the inscribed date travels with the design, not the object.
Whereabouts

An edition multiple — impressions survive, the block does not.

HolderImpressions in institutional collections; the surrogate reproduced here is from the 1592 Progymnasmata prospectus
Evidenceedition multiple extant as impressions; the block untraced
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

Census: (1) Copenhagen, KB, prospectus quaternion, barcode 130023736617 — woodcut verified; (2) Marburg, HStAM Best. 3 II Nr. 1070 (Arcinsys 10465709) — woodcut verified by direct image examination, July 2026; (3) Berlin, SBB, Sammelband Oi 1602 (K10plus PPN 354642936, record live July 2026) — carrier extant, woodcut leaf inferred from Nielsen 1921; (4) Königsberg, UB, Johannes Müller working copy — 'destroyed' per Norlind 1970 (p. 164), unverified since; successor collections (Toruń/Vilnius/Kaliningrad) unchecked. Design dated 1586 in the block; in use on the prospectus title verso distributed 20 Feb – 22 March 1592.

The Prague panel — Mortensen's Clementinum portrait

lifetimeattestedCicon-prague-panel
1586–1592 (design family)
A portrait of Tycho painted on wood, which Mortensen (1946) placed in the Clementinum's astronomical museum at Prague. His passage cites no source and uses no first-hand autopsy language, and no other record from Studnička's Prager Tychoniana (1901) to a dated 2026 sweep of the Czech collections portal corroborates the panel's existence. Its correspondence with the 1586 woodcut rests solely on Mortensen's five itemized points: the same inscription (position confirmed on the reproduction, text illegible), bareheaded with sparse hair (confirmed), the prosthetic nose indicated (claimed by him, not visible on the sheet), the Order of the Elephant on a heavy double chain (a chain is visible; single or double, and the pendant, are unresolvable), and the rings on the hands (untestable). The heraldic apparatus, which Mortensen never claimed as a correspondence, diverges in kind: a single mantled crest, not the woodcut's conserved arch of sixteen ancestral shields, and no beret is present. Mortensen read the panel as the woodcut's painted source rather than its descendant. It was reproduced as a photogravure by the Photographische Gesellschaft in Berlin — a sheet lettered 'Jeremias Falck sculpsit'. Genuine Falck engravings of Tycho exist — Rijksmuseum RP-P-OB-50.565 of 1644, Block 214 / Hollstein Dutch 214, described by Friis in 1890 and exhibited at the Rundetårn tercentenary in 1937 as W.1431 — so the lettering is not wrong for want of a Falck print of Tycho; it is judged erroneous because the sheet, compared at full native resolution, matches the located Falck engraving on no diagnostic point, and because its continuous painterly tonality is that of a reproduced painting rather than an engraving. A further, unlocated Falck plate is not formally excluded. Wellcome's own catalogue nonetheless titles the sheet 'Photogravure after J. Falck' (after Burgess 1973, no. 405.7). The image shown here is that photogravure sheet, and the identification of what it reproduces rests on Mortensen's description rather than on any inscription of its own: it matches him on publisher, on the Falck lettering, on reproducing a painting, and on the checkable points of his description, and faint lettering is visible at the upper left where he places the inscription. Not yet examined at object level in Prague, and no live Czech institutional record has been reached. Mortensen had, in 1942, dismissed two oil portraits at the Prague Observatory as worthless eighteenth-century copies after de Gheyn; he treats those oils and the Clementinum panel as separate objects but never reconciles the two accounts, and both stated locations trace to the same institutional complex. A third strand runs earlier still: Bergeron's 1603 account of the Belvedere memorial records two paintings of Tycho there, untraced after the collection moved in 1635 (Fučíková 2002) — lost painted Tychos in Prague are multiply attested, a base rate that fits several distinct objects and anchors none of them.
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
A painted portrait on wood attested only by Mortensen (1946). Correspondence with the 1586 woodcut is established solely on his five itemized points (inscription position confirmed, bareheaded confirmed, nose claimed but not visible, chain unresolvable, rings untestable); the heraldic apparatus diverges in kind (single mantled crest vs the 16-shield arch) and no beret is present. Mortensen read it as the woodcut's model; direction of dependence unsettled, and the existence question is now logically prior.
Dated 1586 → open
The panel, painted on wood and held in the astronomical museum in the Clementinum at Prague, is unsigned and carries the same composition and the same inscription as the 1586 woodcut, so the two are certainly connected. Which came first is the question. Mortensen argued the panel is the woodcut's model rather than a copy of it, resting chiefly on a detail of the hands: the painting shows two rings that the woodcut leaves out, and a copyist adds detail rather than removing it. If he is right the panel dates from about 1586 and is the closest surviving thing to the painted stage behind the whole print campaign; if he is wrong the panel could be considerably later. He was himself tentative, and the record follows him without asserting him. Two further limits now stand on that question. The ring count — the sole load-bearing evidence for the model thesis — is untestable at the surviving reproduction's confirmed native maximum of 2200 × 3190 px: two independent attempts left no countable ring bands on either hand, so Mortensen's evidence is currently unverifiable in either direction. And since the panel's existence is itself attested by Mortensen alone, the existence question is logically prior to the dating question.
Whereabouts

Reported in the astronomical museum of the Clementinum at Prague; not confirmed against a live Czech record.

HolderUnlocated; Mortensen (1946) placed it in the Clementinum's astronomical museum. The Mathematical Museum was dissolved in 1785; Národní knihovna ČR (the Klementinum's current occupant) could not be reached and does not participate in eSbírky.cz., Prague
Evidenceattested attested — the Prague location rests on Mortensen; no institutional record has been reached
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

The plate reproduced here is the Wellcome Collection's photogravure (1355i / V0000746), lettered, in error, 'Jeremias Falck sculps.', Public Domain Mark. The plate number on the sheet reads 8279 at native resolution, matching the Wellcome catalogue; Mortensen's printed caption apparently reads 3279 (two OCR passes, possibly of a shared ancestor scan) — an unresolved discrepancy, recorded rather than silently resolved.

Where the sources disagree 4
QuestionPositionsStatus
Mortensen's citation for a Strahov portraitMortensen cites Studnička, Prager Tychoniana (1901), for a Strahov portrait. Studnička catalogues four manuscripts, six printed books and three works of art, and contains no painted portrait panel; the only Strahov portrait image he reproduces is the gilt supralibros on the Mechanica presentation binding.serious. If Mortensen's citation is the sole basis for this panel's provenance, that basis needs re-examining
Wellcome's cataloguing vs the Falck comparisonThe Wellcome catalogue titles the sheet 'Photogravure after J. Falck' (after Burgess 1973, no. 405.7), taking the lettering at face value. Direct comparison at full native resolution shows the sheet does not reproduce the located Falck engraving of Tycho (Rijksmuseum RP-P-OB-50.565, 1644, Block 214 / Hollstein Dutch 214): a different composition on every diagnostic point, and continuous painterly tonality with none of an engraving's line structure. A further, unlocated Falck plate is not formally excluded.resolved against the face-value title on the evidence available; Burgess's own entry remains unexamined
Mortensen 1942 vs Mortensen 1946In 1942 Mortensen dismissed two oil portraits at the Prague Observatory as worthless eighteenth-century copies after de Gheyn (Westergaard 1377 and 1394); in 1946 he presented a Clementinum panel as possibly the 1586 woodcut's painted model. He treats the objects as separate but never reconciles the two accounts, and the two stated locations trace to the same institutional complex.open — an unacknowledged tension within Mortensen's own testimony
The prosthetic noseMortensen states 'den falske Naese er angivet' — the false nose is indicated. No prosthesis seam or discontinuity is visible on the sheet at any available resolution, including the confirmed native maximum.open — a resolution limit of the photogravure, or an over-reading by Mortensen
Open questions 7
  1. No Czech institutional record for the panel has been reached — Klementinum, Národní technické muzeum, or the National Library's own catalogue.
  2. The Photographische Gesellschaft's own series catalogue or caption for plate 8279 — what the Berlin firm said the sheet reproduces — is the most decisive source not yet consulted.
  3. The entry text of Burgess 1973, no. 405.7 — the only named scholarly authority behind Wellcome's 'after J. Falck' title — remains unread.
  4. Hollstein's Falck entries, and Strunk's catalogue cited by Friis, have not been checked for a second Tycho plate beyond Block 214.
  5. A page-image read of Mortensen's printed plate number (Cassiopeia 8, p. 62) to settle 3279 vs 8279 at the print level.
  6. The Národní muzeum album-amicorum watercolour (Wittenberg 1598) as a possible referent behind a 'Prague painted portrait' citation — Studnička's only painted likeness.
  7. Westergaard vol. I, pp. 200 ff. (entries 1383–1431), remains to be read in full, for the text of W.1394 and the Falck entry W.1431.

8

The mural quadrant portrait (Uraniborg)

lifetimelostAicon-mural-portrait
1586–1587 (tablet: 1587)
Painted in 1587 within the arc of the great mural quadrant: a trompe-l'oeil 'diorama' in which Tycho, seated life-size in effigy in a fur-lined robe whose front carries a gold frog-and-button closure — no order insignia or pendant of any kind — looks up and points to the real observing-slit cut in the room's south wall, a dog (fidelity) at his feet and an open book with dividers and square on the table before him. Through the illusory wall one sees into the three storeys of the south tower — the vaulted laboratory, the library with the great brass globe, and the observatory with working assistants — flanked by medallion portraits of Frederik II and Queen Sofie; a tablet records it as the work of Tycho, 'author of the building and the astronomical instruments', in his 40th year. The painting perished with Uraniborg and survives through the copperplate engraving in the Mechanica (1598); a re-cut woodblock derivative of the plate appears in the 1666 Historia Coelestis (Liber Prolegomenon, p. cxiii).
Maker
Tobias Gemperlin (figure) and Hans van Stenwinkel the Elder (architecture), both named by Tycho in the Mechanica; Hans Knieper (landscape) — Tycho names only 'Johannes of Antwerp, royal painter at Kronborg', and the Knieper identification is uncontested scholarship (Friis; Christianson 2000)
Original or copy
Original destroyed. A painting executed directly on the west wall of the south-west ground-floor room at Uraniborg; it perished with the building. It survives only through the engraved plate in the Mechanica (1598).
Fate
the painting lost with Uraniborg; survives only in the Mechanica engraving
Dated 1582 → 1587
The great mural quadrant itself was built in 1582 — nothing could be painted in its arc before that. The date 1587 is not merely conventional: it is inscribed on the plate's own tablet ('A° DOMINI 1587 ÆTATIS SVÆ 40', confirmed letter-for-letter at 600 DPI in two independent KB Copenhagen copies), and Tycho's text endorses the tablet ('prout vides'). The cap on Gemperlin's share of the work comes from his death — dead by 1587, the die-window running 1586 to May 1588 — not from Tycho's testimony. For the effigy and the architecture the attribution rests on Tycho's own word: he named Gemperlin and Stenwinkel outright, and praised the likeness in the Mechanica ('vix similior dari possit' — scarcely could a more faithful one be made); the landscape hand he named only as 'Johannes of Antwerp, royal painter at Kronborg', the surname Knieper being inherited, uncontested scholarship. A genuine late-1586 strand — Gassendi (via Resen 1668, attached to this mural effigy specifically), Thoren 1990, and Friis's earlier essay ('omtrent 1586') — dates the execution to late 1586; under aetatis-40 reckoning (Tycho's 40th year ran 14 December 1586 – 13 December 1587) both dates are consistent with a sitting late in 1586 completed by 1587.
Whereabouts

Destroyed with Uraniborg in the early 17th century. No painted fragment is attested anywhere.

PlaceSite only: Uraniborg, island of Ven, Skåne, Sweden
Evidencedestroyed destroyed
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

Hven passed in fief to Cort Barleben in May 1602 and the demolition proceeded for want of anyone to look after it; the replacement house was built from Uraniborg's bricks, a mason being paid in 1623 for 60,000 bricks 'pulled down and renovated from the old castle Oranienborg' (Dreyer 1890, Appendix p. 376). Gone without trace by Huet's 1652 visit; Picard found only foundations in 1671 (Dreyer 1890, Appendix pp. 376–377). No painted fragment appears in any excavation record, 1814–1973.

Relations
  • depicts Tycho Brahe
  • painted within Quadrans muralis sive Tichonicus — the great mural quadrant
  • appears in Mechanica (1598)
  • painted by Tobias Gemperlin
  • painted by Hans Knieper
  • painted by Hans van Stenwinkel the Elder

9

The gilt portrait supralibros — two dies

lifetimeextantAicon-supralibros
die by 20 March 1588
Tycho's most personal device: a gilt-stamped portrait of himself, bust-length in a cartwheel ruff, wearing the loaded chain scheme of his royal favour — the Frederik II medallion with the initials F.S., an elephant pendant (Danske Magazin 1746 — the earliest eyewitness — reports only 'en Elefant', seen 'tydeligen' in the second chain, and names no order; reading it as the Order of the Elephant insignia is this edition's gloss and Mortensen's 1946 gloss on the model engraving, and it sits badly against the fact that Tycho held no such membership; the photographs alone cannot resolve the object), and a third chain whose ornament no witness has made out — Danske Magazin 1746, p. 322, is the source of that negative: 'om der har været noget Smykke i den tredie, kand man ikke see'. Impressed in gold on the front covers of deluxe presentation bindings (the arms stamp on the back), it branded his gifts with his likeness: on De mundi (1588), the Epistolae (1596), the Mechanica (1598), and on Prague bindings to c.1601–03. Around the portrait runs the motto 'Hic patet exterior Tychonis forma Brahei / pulchrius eniteat, quae latet interior' ('here is Tycho's outward form; may the inner, hidden, shine more beautifully'). The stamp exists in TWO sizes, but the published metrology is shaky throughout: Mortensen 1931 prints the figures as '7,6 X 5,8 og 5,9 X 4,3 mm' — millimetres where centimetres must be meant, and with the small die's width drifting between 5.9 and 5.8 across his 1931 and 1966 articles; Friis gives 8 × 6 cm, on the authority of the earliest published rendering of the large die — the plate at Danske Magazin II (1746), p. 321, engraved by O. H. de Lode from the Royal Library's blue-satin Mechanica (LN 432 2°, eks. 2, the Petr Vok presentation copy) and printed, the accompanying text insists, at the die's own size ('Billedet paa Bogen er i selv samme Størrelse, som dette Koberstykke'); that plate is a witness in its own right, not merely Friis's authority, and it carries the only 1:1 claim in the whole literature; a scaled estimate from the Stockholm RAR: 106 A digitisation (c. 6.3–6.5 × 5.9 cm) matches none of them; and every figure is taken from impressions on compressible supports, never from tools; the smaller die is far the rarer — its documented carrier is the KB Calvin/Theocritus volume given to Hans Lindenov — and it differs tellingly from the large: the small die's ruff edge carries serrated notches, rendering the lobed cartwheel ruff of the engraving family faithfully, where the large die smooths them away (Mortensen noticed the notches; object photographs confirm them). Two facts anchor the die pair's place in the story. First, the large die is the earliest witness of the multi-chain-and-medallion costume — it predates de Gheyn's first engraving, and on the leading reconstruction the costume scheme travelled from Denmark to the Amsterdam workshop, plausibly with the De mundi presentation copies in the hands of Heinrich Rantzau, broker of the engraving commission. Second, the dies were never revised: after the second engraving corrected the costume (c.1595), the binding stamps went on impressing the old loaded scheme for the rest of Tycho's life and posthumously — the correction was aimed at the print's public circulation, not at the livery of his bindings.
Maker
Cutter unrecorded. With the die now dated before late March 1588, Gemperlin (dead by 1587) is possible as designer and Hans Crol, Tycho's goldsmith on Hven (d. 30 November 1591), as cutter — but these are names of convenience, neither documented. The die pair's relationship is genuinely open: 1597 is the small die's first documented use, not a cutting date. If it is a later cutting it needs another hand; but Friis (c. 1890) held the small die the older of the pair — undefended, and the shared design cannot by itself discriminate one commission from two cuttings off one pattern.
Original or copy
The DIE is untraced — no source places either physical tool in any collection, and the two published sizes (7.6 × 5.8 cm and 5.8 × 4.3 cm) are Mortensen's measurements of impressions, not of tools. What survives is the stamped bindings, which are originals.
Dated late 1586 → 20 March 1588
The upper bound is an object: the Royal Library's Sascerides copy of De mundi aetherei (LN 428 4°, eks. 1) carries both gilt ovals — portrait on the front cover, arms on the back — on its original parchment presentation binding, with Tycho's autograph dedication to Gellius Sascerides dated 20 March 1588 (verified on the EEB surrogate), so both dies existed by that date. The British Library's Maestlin copy, dispatched from Uraniborg on 14 May 1588, remains the second keystone — nobody rebinds a book into a Tycho presentation binding. The lower bound is the costume it carries: the multi-chain-and-medallion scheme belongs to the likeness layer begun with the 1586 sitting. This dating overturns the older literature's assumption (Mortensen; Christianson) that the stamp copies de Gheyn's engraving — the engraving was cut only in autumn 1590, two years after the die was in use. The small die's own first anchor is much later: its documented carrier is the Calvin/Theocritus volume Tycho gave Hans Lindenov, registered at Uraniborg on 21 March 1597.
Whereabouts

Neither die survives. The impressions survive on presentation and library bindings across Europe.

HolderBest-documented example: Det Kongelige Bibliotek, Copenhagen, Copenhagen
InventoryLN 432 2°, eks. 2 — Tycho's presentation copy of the Mechanica to Petr Vok z Rožmberka
Evidencelocated located (KB's own digitisation photographs the stamped upper cover at leaf LN_432_2_000)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

The die's survival is a documented negative, not an unchecked gap: Gigas 1900 describes twelve KB bindings tool by tool and mentions no tool; Mortensen 1931/1966, Studnička 1901, Kleinschnitzová 1933 and Voit's Encyklopedie knihy all describe stamped covers only. Sixteenth-century binder's tools essentially never survive.

Copy-level carriers

Portrait supralibros documented on: the KB Sascerides copy (LN 428 4°, eks. 1, autograph dedication 20 March 1588 — now the earliest dated carrier), the BL Maestlin De mundi (dispatched 14 May 1588 — the second dating keystone), the Magini De mundi, KB LN 428 copy 2, the Stockholm KB De mundi (RAR: 106 A), the Strahov Mechanica (green satin), the Wolfenbüttel Epistolae (HAB Astronomica 8), the St Petersburg Otto Brahe Mechanica (dedicated 5 October 1598), the Cambrai Lobkowitz Mechanica (dedicated 18 August 1600), and posthumous bindings to c.1603. Not the Smithsonian Epistolae, whose cover carries the arms stamp.

Settled
  1. The Petr Vok dedication date — settled at 18 September 1600. It was never a three-way: the September reading is the object itself, Tycho's autograph 'Anno 1600 Septembris Die 18' on leaf LN_432_2_003 of the Royal Library's digitisation (Digitale Samlinger object11914), read at full resolution in July 2026 (see the Mechanica watercolours record). Langebek's 'd. 18. Decemb. 1600' (Danske Magazin II, 1746, p. 321) is a misreading of the same flyleaf 146 years later, and the '1599?' sometimes attached to the copy has no stated basis.
Open questions 5
  1. Does either physical die survive? Unchecked channels are Nationalmuseet's object database and Frederiksborg's iconographic register, neither of which is likely to hold a binding tool. The documentary silence now runs back past Gigas to Resen 1668 and Danske Magazin 1746 — stamped covers described, never a tool.
  2. Per-volume die-size assignment for Kleinschnitzová's six Clementinum shelfmarks (8 H 39, 12 H 10, 14 G 30, 14 J 176, 19 C 92, 9 B 135) — the decisive test of the format hypothesis, with predictions on record (octavos small, quartos large).
  3. Reconciliation of the Bodleian SOLO full-livery description of the Savile copy against the photographed 4° T 2 Art.Seld. Sammelband, and recovery of the Weston Savilian shelfmark — one object with a misattached description, or a seventh De mundi carrier?
  4. A first-hand raking-light reading of both gilt legends on the Sascerides copy (LN 428 4°, eks. 1), illegible in the digitised surrogate.
  5. A caliper reading of the de Lode plate. Danske Magazin II (1746) claims to render the portrait die at 1:1 and the arms die at 'selv samme Form og Størrelse'; measuring the oval on a physical 1746 sheet — or on a scan with a known scale — is the cheapest independent test of the large die's dimensions anywhere in this edition, and the only one that does not measure a compressed impression on leather or vellum.

10

Frederik II's funeral procession, 1588 — Tycho among the coffin-bearers

lifetimeextantCicon-funeral-procession-1588
5 June 1588 (the event); plate 1589
The engraved suite of Frederik II's funeral cortege. Tycho is said to have walked as number 7 in the left-hand row of the 32 bearers, between his brother Knud (no. 3) and his future brother-in-law Erik Lange (no. 11) — though the plate legend has the nobles bearing the canopy (tentorium) over the bier, not the coffin itself; 'bore the coffin' is the 2006 caption's compression. This would be the only documentary depiction of Tycho taking part in a Danish state occasion during his lifetime — he was 41 — but his physical presence at Roskilde on 5 June 1588 is unestablished: the Uraniborg diary notes 'Sepultura Regis' with no travel note for Tycho, against its own convention of logging his journeys.
Maker
Frans Hogenberg and Simon Novellanus, Cologne; commissioned by Heinrich Rantzau.
Original or copy
An original engraved suite. But this is a positional claim, not a likeness — and the position itself is fragile. Direct full-resolution examination of the Kiel sheet (Yb 54-15) found no numerals and no name-list on the plate face, only the sheet number '15' and two royal captions; the legend 'Tentorium supra funus extulerunt nobiles sequentes' with its name column ('Canutus Brahe' legible; 'Tycho' never yet recovered from it) appears in the impression the 2006 volume photographed (Det Kongelige Bibliotek) and cannot currently be located on the Kiel sheet. The numbering's period basis is unestablished, and the 2006 book itself says outright that he cannot be picked out in the picture — 'uden at man dog sikkert kan udpege ham på billedet'.
Dated 5 June 1588 → 1589
Frederik II died on 4 April 1588 and the procession to Roskilde took place on 5 June; the engraved suite is dated 1589.
Whereabouts

An edition multiple. A complete 16-sheet set is at Kiel.

HolderSchleswig-Holsteinische Landesbibliothek, Kiel, Germany
InventoryYb 54-15 (within the set Yb 54)
Evidencelocated located (record-level LIDO page + CC BY-SA 4.0 full-resolution IIIF surrogate, examined directly: https://mab.digicult-verbund.de/digicult/rsc/iiif/image/Iview/digicult_derivate_00311870%2FYb%2054-15.JPG/full/full/0/default.jpg)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

Further impressions at Augsburg (SuStB 2 Kst 180, digitised). Det Kongelige Bibliotek holds Rantzau's hand-coloured presentation copy to Prince Christian (IV) — the impression the 2006 book photographed, though which object that photograph actually reproduces is now an open question (see conflicts). Three presentation copies carry Rantzau gift-inscriptions dated 15 May 1590: Manderup Parsberg's (later Thore Virgin, Bibliotheca Qvarnforsiana, Uppsala 1946, no. 41; current whereabouts unknown), Christian IV's, and Duke Hans den Yngre's (Ilsøe, Fund og Forskning 44 (2005), p. 505, reviewing Zeeberg). Tycho's own copy was sent to him by Rantzau at the end of 1588 together with Civitates Orbis Terrarum Liber IV (Grinder-Hansen 2006, p. 222). Zeeberg's Rantzau bibliography deliberately excludes the Res gestae as a pure picture series ('ren billedserie') — which is why no numbered bibliography assigns it a number.

Where the sources disagree 1
QuestionPositionsStatus
The title'Typus Pompae Funebris' — the 2006 book. No consulted catalogue uses that title: the plate's engraved heading is 'Pompa funebris habita Rodtschildij…', and the containing work is catalogued as Res gestae … Friderici II (1589).'Typus Pompae Funebris' is a genuine period title — BM P_1870-0514-2874 (1588 print) and BnF ark:/12148/cb32491664b (folio prepared by Rantzau, dedicated to Christian IV) — most plausibly of the 1588 funeral print series (per Lohmeier: 21 copperplates by Hogenberg alone, accompanying the letterpress Descriptio), distinct from the 1589 16-plate Res gestae in which the Kiel funeral plate is sheet 15. The open question is now which object Grinder-Hansen photographed.

11

De Gheyn's first engraving (Westergaard 1377) — the 'overloaded' portrait

lifetimeextantAicon-degheyn-v1
autumn 1590, Amsterdam
The first copperplate portrait: Tycho bareheaded within a triumphal arch of sixteen ancestral quarterings, the beret lying on the parapet, wearing the Frederik II portrait-medal and stacked chains — the loaded scheme the gilt binding die had carried since 1588. Whether that scheme reached the plate as an image transmitted from Denmark (plausibly with the De mundi presentation copies Heinrich Rantzau held) or was composed in Amsterdam under de Gheyn's documented house procedure — the model supplying the head, with costume, frame, arms and lettering engraved directly into the plate — is undecided; the commission's route is likewise unattested: Beckett & Christensen's 1921 conjecture that Rantzau brokered it has no documentary support and was never adopted by the print literature. Tycho reacted at once: on 7 December 1590 he complained to Rantzau that the portrait was overloaded with chains ('nimiis monilibus onustae') against the plainer model he had supplied, and ordered a replacement the same day. Forensic registration of impressions (2026) proved that the state-I and 'Marco Sadeler excudit' impressions in the Rijksmuseum are pulls of one plate — the Sadeler impressions are posthumous restrikes, not a new plate, the plate itself having passed to the Sadeler publishing stock from Tycho's estate. Whether the large-format impression printed integrally in a Copenhagen 1602 Progymnasmata copy and the smaller SMK impression come from that same plate remains open, awaiting plate-mark and raking-light comparison (a three-witness flaw comparison was negative but non-diagnostic, and water-staining of the Copenhagen leaf offers a paper-distortion alternative for the size differential). That 1602 title-verso impression is the engraving's only physically verified book use; the portraits found in five examined digitized Epistolae astronomicae copies are all the second engraving, to which New Hollstein assigns the Epistolae use — the insertions were loose, so copy-level variation among unexamined copies is not excluded. The plate carries the heraldic errors later corrected in the second engraving — BELKER standing where the Vasa sheaf belonged, HØNNOR for Rønnow — a list the 2026 shield-by-shield re-examination confirms complete at three (the VLFSTANDER, HØNNOR and BELKER shields; the thirteen others identical in both states) — and no depiction of the nose prosthesis or of any duel scar. The cartouche wording also separates the two engravings even on low-resolution digitizations: 'Machinarumque … Dispositarum' here, 'Instrumentorumque … Dispositorum' in the second plate.
Maker
Jacques de Gheyn II (Antwerp 1565 – The Hague 1629), signed in the plate; an independent Amsterdam engraver by 1588, previously trained in Goltzius's Haarlem workshop. No document names Goltzius anywhere in the commission — the old account that the commission 'went to Goltzius, who passed it to his pupil' is Beckett & Christensen's 1921 conjecture, built on premises (a dependent pupil; engravings of 1586) that are both refuted.
Original or copy
An original plate by de Gheyn, signed. Impressions are originals; later states carry Marcus Sadeler's address.
Dated autumn 1590 → 1 December 1590
The tightest-dated object in the whole series, on Tycho's own letters: 'nuper Amsterodami cupro insculptae' — recently engraved at Amsterdam — he wrote on 1 December 1590, dispatching impressions to Thomas Savile, with further sendings to Rantzau (7 December) and Scultetus (8 December). The letters name no artist; the plate is signed by de Gheyn. The inscribed 'aetatis 40 … 1586' names the likeness's origin, not the plate's date.
Whereabouts

An edition multiple. New Hollstein records state I (before the address) at Amsterdam (Rijksmuseum), Coburg, Dresden, Leiden, London, Rotterdam and Wolfegg, and state II ('Marco Sadeler excudit') at Amsterdam, Coburg, Haarlem, London, New York, two Paris collections (one the Fondation Custodia) and Vienna. Accessions verified against the museums' own records: Rijksmuseum RP-P-OB-9945 (state I, 192 × 138 mm) and RP-P-1894-A-18638 (state II, 188 × 137 mm, acquired 1894); Metropolitan Museum of Art 62.650.203 (state II, 191 × 140 mm); Art Institute of Chicago 2023.153 (state II, 190 × 140 mm). The Copenhagen impressions:

HolderStatens Museum for Kunst, Den Kongelige Kobberstiksamling, Copenhagen
InventoryKKS4530 (state I, no address); KKSgb7575 (state II, 'Marco Sadeler excudit')
Evidencelocated located (live SMK API records with IIIF images)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

KKS4530: 186 × 134 mm, signed 'J.d. Gheyn.Fe', purchased 1903 from C. G. Boerner, Leipzig. KKSgb7575: the structured dimensions read 187 × 142 mm; the record's note field repeats KKS4530's '186 × 134 mm' verbatim, probably an inherited copy-paste (187 × 142 preferred, unconfirmed by SMK). SMK dates both sheets 1588–1590, departing from New Hollstein's c. 1595 — a departure the museum's own notes source to an e-mail from Christian Westergaard of 31 January 2025; SMK's separate point estimate, 'ca. 1590', converges with the autumn-1590 date fixed by Tycho's letters. No institution publishes a plate-mark or arch-span measurement for any impression.

Elsewhere in Tychoniana Progymnasmata

12

The Copenhagen pen drawing (SMK KKSgb5254) — the second engraving's production drawing

lifetimeextantAicon-drawing-kksgb5254
c. 1591–1595
The only early production drawing of the Tycho portraits to survive: pen and brown ink, 122 × 91 mm, bought by the print cabinet in 1810 from the heirs of Lorenz Spengler. Tycho in the worn plumed beret, head finished in disciplined engraving-idiom hatching, costume summary — exactly the workshop method (the model supplies the head; the rest was engraved directly on the plate). It is the mirror of the second engraving with the head transferring at 1:1: the physical signature of a sheet traced face-up onto the copperplate. Its costume differs from both engravings (a taller plumed hat, an open radiating collar, two chains), which rules out every copy-after-print pathway — a copyist reproduces his source's costume. The lost companion design for the first engraving is recorded by Altena (his cat. 653). The verso carries an old ink 'Thicho Brahe' inscription and the faint red-chalk 'HG' monogram that both the museum and the de Gheyn catalogue judge forged. The Goltzius attribution's documented trail is: the pre-1890s common opinion (Friis records no verso inscription at all, only the Spengler stamp), the museum's own Goltzius display label in 1921 (Beckett & Christensen, who argued instead for Gemperlin), Mortensen's 1946 report of the name Goltzius on the verso, and Altena's 1983 'false monogram HG'. Whether Mortensen's 'name' and the modern 'HG' initials describe the same physical mark is unresolved; a documented parallel for such an upgrade exists (the collector Ploos van Amstel added exactly such a false 'HG' to de Gheyn's Grotius drawing to sell it as Goltzius).
Maker
Jacques de Gheyn II is the best-supported hand (the pen medium is his documented practice for portrait-print models — 'Goltzius never did' so, per Reznicek — and the sheet's idiom matches his secure Marnix model); a residual alternative is an unidentified copyist supplying the model. Tobias Gemperlin is excluded as draughtsman (dead by 1587, and no secure Gemperlin drawing exists to compare). The old Goltzius attribution is refuted for authorship (no Tycho in Reznicek's corpus, wrong medium, Goltzius in Italy from late 1590); its documentary trail is a pre-1890s common opinion — still the museum's displayed label in 1921 — revived by Mortensen 1946 on the strength of a verso mark first recorded that year (Mortensen: the name Goltzius; Altena/SMK: a red-chalk 'HG' monogram) and judged forged by both the museum and the de Gheyn catalogue.
Original or copy
An original working drawing — the only early production drawing of the Tycho portraits to survive.
Dated after 7 December 1590 → c. mid-1595
The drawing is the working model for the second engraving — mirror-reversed against the print, needle-traced for transfer, the head at 1:1 with the engraved head (measured) — so it postdates the 7 December 1590 order for that engraving and predates the plate's completion, which Lambert Cornelisz's dated 1595 copy places by mid-1595. The 'ca. 1586?' sometimes attached to it is back-dating driven by the untenable Gemperlin attribution; the sheet's design content (the 1586 likeness of the 40-year-old) is older than the sheet. A residual alternative — one c. 1590 sheet serving both plates, v1's head also transferred from it — remains live and unexcluded: both engravings carry the identical AETATIS-40/1586 caption, and the documented v1→v2 manner shift (dense/tonal → lighter/linear) could make a single model read smoother in v1 and more furrowed in v2. The drawing's v2-matching age signature and worn cap are supporting but confounded observations (low–medium); the discriminating test — calibrated 1:1 scale registration of the head against NH 238 — has not been run.
Whereabouts

In the Royal Collection of Graphic Art, Copenhagen.

HolderStatens Museum for Kunst, Den Kongelige Kobberstiksamling, Copenhagen
InventoryKKSgb5254
Evidencelocated located (live SMK record)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

Pen and brown ink, 122 × 91 mm; bought in 1810 from the heirs of Lorenz Spengler (Lugt 1763). Mirror-reversed against the second engraving, needle-traced for transfer, the head at 1:1 with the engraved head. The verso red-chalk 'HG' monogram is recorded by SMK as probably a forgery.
