13

De Gheyn's second engraving (Westergaard 1394) — the corrected portrait

lifetimeextantAicon-degheyn-v2
ordered December 1590; design fixed by mid-1595; plate completed by 1596
The corrected replacement, and the portrait by which Europe knew Tycho: aged now, in the worn plumed beret, within the reworked arch. A complete change-register against the first engraving (22 verified differences, July 2026) shows a deliberately corrective new plate: the Frederik II medal removed and the stacked chains reduced to a single doubled chain (executing the 1590 complaint), the collar changed from standing to flat, the doublet opened — the whole dress more modest; the heraldic errors fixed (RØNNOR corrected, the Vasa wheat-sheaf cut where BELKER's bars had stood); the tablet's vocabulary revised from 'machinarum' to 'instrumentorum'; the sitter's face updated to the man of nearly fifty while the tablet keeps 'aetatis 40 … 1586', the caption of the likeness's origin; the hands transposed left-for-right while the head is not mirrored — the fingerprint of a freshly drawn plate, produced from the surviving Copenhagen drawing by direct needle transfer. The ornate pendant jewel over an elephant form survives from the first plate — the one costume element never revised. The plate faced the 1596 Epistolae; the engraving is recorded present in a documented copy of the 1610 Frankfurt-titled reissue (Amsterdam UvA OG 8°-2(1)), but that issue is unsold 1596 sheets under a new title-leaf — no fresh 1610 press-run of the plate is evidenced, and the Hülsius/Tampach lineage's documented portrait supply was a copy plate.
Maker
Jacques de Gheyn II, signed in the plate ('Geyn. fe').
Original or copy
An original plate by de Gheyn, signed 'Geyn. fe'. The corrected replacement for the first engraving.
Dated 7 December 1590 (ordered) → mid-1595 (design attested in Tycho's hands via the Cornelisz copy); plate completion by end 1596 (Altena's outer bound)
The lower bound is Tycho's own re-order in the complaint letter of 7 December 1590 ('aliam illic confieri curem' — I am having another made there). The upper bound (revised July 2026): Lambert Cornelisz's reversed copy of this portrait is dated 1595 in its plate and was in Tycho's hands by 5 September 1595 (his letter to Pontanus) — so the design existed by mid-1595. Whether Cornelisz copied the printed plate or the Vorlage drawing KKSgb5254 is contested (Mortensen 1946 p. 21: 'Forlæg: Pennetegningen'); the 1595 date therefore pins the design with high confidence and the plate's completion only probably — the hard ceiling for the finished plate is Altena's 'before the end of 1596'. This still retires the older account that dated the model-delivery to May 1596 (the painter in Tycho's diary on 4 May and Blaeu's departure on 24 May 1596 cannot explain a design that was already circulating in Cornelisz's copy the year before). The corrected heraldry gives an internal lower bound as well: the error-state was still circulating on the February–March 1592 prospectus, so the correction falls between 1592 and the plate's completion (by end 1596).
Whereabouts

An edition multiple; NH locates impressions at Amsterdam, Dresden, Paris, Wolfegg (single state). The NH-illustrated Amsterdam impression is Rijksmuseum RP-P-OB-9945 (192 × 138 mm sheet vs 187 × 135 mm plate, Public Domain) — preferred full-margin surrogate candidate; SMK KKSgb7084 (186 × 132 mm) is trimmed by at most 1–3 mm, within impression variance.

HolderStatens Museum for Kunst, Den Kongelige Kobberstiksamling, Copenhagen
InventoryKKSgb7084
Evidencelocated located (live SMK record; the museum's own documentation block cites Grinder-Hansen 2006 and states that this sheet is the one reproduced as fig. 7)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

186 × 132 mm, signed 'Geyn. fe' (cursive ligatured initial) in the field left of the sitter at chest height, beside the heraldic pilaster; the lower-right field is empty; pencil collector's mark 'MR.C.M.DOZY' lower left, acquisition 1787. New Hollstein 239; Westergaard 1394. SMK dates it 1594–95.

Copy-level carriers

Four digitised copies examined across all three issue-clusters (July 2026): every portrait present is the hatted second engraving — Smithsonian/Dibner (1596 issue, hand-coloured), ETH Zürich (1601 Hülsius issue), Florence BNCF (1610 Tampach issue); the Dresden SLUB 1610-issue copy lacks the leaf entirely (loose inserts do go missing). Nielsen §430 names no state ('et kobberstukket Portræt'). No copy carrying the capless first engraving has yet been found (n=4 of 29 census copies).

Elsewhere in Tychoniana Epistolae astronomicae (1596) · census copy smithsonian-dibner of epistolae 1596

14

Lambert Cornelisz's print (Westergaard 1409) — the copy Tycho disliked

lifetimeextantAicon-cornelisz-1595
1595, Amsterdam
A small Amsterdam trade engraving: Tycho bust-length in the plumed beret within an oval inscribed 'TYCHO BRAHE OTTONIDES DAN. ASTRONOMOR HVIVS SÆCVLI PRINCEPS 1595', his hand resting on two celestial hemispheres with a small armillary sphere between them — the engraver's own additions to a reversed copy of de Gheyn's second portrait. Tycho neither ordered nor liked it. His 5 September 1595 letter objects to the hemispheres ('Non satis placet, quod duobus hemisphaeriis imaginem istam inniti fecerit artifex' — it has less gravity) and to the epithet 'Princeps astronomorum' as too pompous an honour — but says not a word against the hat: the same editorial control he had exercised over the chains in 1590, tacitly accepting the hatted scheme. The print is rare; no institutional impression could be enumerated online for this edition, and the recorded apparatus is the Danish catalogue (Westergaard 1409), the descriptive primary account in Friis, Bidrag til dansk Kunsthistorie, p. 228 (the Kunstindustrimuseet impression, 9.2 × 7.8 cm, trimmed with the signature cut away; the 1868 Vienna sighting noted as 'L. Cornay'), and the trimmed reproduction in Friis 1902 (Nogle Efterretninger, per Mortensen).
Maker
Lambert Cornelisz (Amsterdam engraver of portraits and title pages, active 1593–1621 per CERL cnp02079345, which records 'Cornely' among his attested name variants and de Gheyn II as his teacher); the reproduced untrimmed impression carries a legible 'L Corney … fecit'; Friis's 'Cornay' is a contemporary 1868 note on a Vienna impression published 1902, and Mortensen's 'Cornely' derives from Friis's reproduction of the signature-less Copenhagen exemplar, not an independent reading; made on the engraver's own initiative, not commissioned by Tycho.
Original or copy
An original plate by Lambert Cornelisz — but a derivative image: a reversed copy of de Gheyn's second portrait, made on the engraver's own initiative and disliked by Tycho.
Dated 1595 (dated in the plate) → 5 September 1595 (in Tycho's hands)
The plate carries its own date, 1595, and Tycho's letter to his former assistant Pontanus, written at Uraniborg on 5 September 1595, shows he had already received impressions — one had come earlier via Cort Aslaksson, who wrongly believed it engraved at Cologne. The letter is also the print's significance for the whole chronology: being a reversed copy of the second de Gheyn portrait, its date proves the v2 image existed in copyable form by mid-1595; it dates the finished v2 plate only on the leading (unproven) reading that Cornelisz copied a printed impression rather than the design — the diagnostic zones are unusable because Cornelisz replaced the background apparatus, the mirror evidence is mixed, and CERL records de Gheyn II as his teacher (undated), a possible access route to pre-plate material. Under the design-model reading the plate's own terminus loosens and the May-1596 Blaeu/Pictor channel reopens.
Whereabouts

An edition multiple; the surrogate reproduced here is a portrait-archive impression whose holding institution is not yet confirmed.

Evidenceedition multiple extant as impressions; holder of the reproduced sheet unconfirmed
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

Westergaard 1409. Tycho had received impressions by 5 September 1595 (letter to Pontanus). Three impressions attested, none currently confirmed live: (1) Kunstindustrimuseet Copenhagen (Friis, trimmed, signature cut away) — successor Designmuseum Danmark, prints registry online H2 2026; (2) Vienna k.k. Hofbibliothek, seen 1868 — continues as ÖNB, Porträtsammlung candidate GZ00337643 unresolved; (3) the reproduced sheet — untrimmed, signed 'L Corney … fecit', holder unconfirmed, possibly = the Vienna impression. BM, Rijksmuseum and SMK negatives logged.

Open questions 7
  1. The holding institution of the impression reproduced here is unidentified.
  2. Whether the ÖNB Porträtsammlung's GZ00337643 is the impression Friis records as seen in Vienna in 1868 — a question for the Bildarchiv.
  3. Whether the ex-Kunstindustrimuseet impression described by Friis survives at Designmuseum Danmark.
  4. Westergaard I:204, entry 1409, has not been read at first hand; no digitisation exists.
  5. Friis's promised side-by-side reproductions of the print and KKSgb5254 — the best extant lever on the plate-versus-design question — have not been located.
  6. Friis's 'gloves in the left hand' reading of the pose has not been checked against KKSgb5254.
  7. The 'Non satis placet…' quotation has not been collated verbatim against TBOO VII, p. 373.

15

The 1595 portrait medal — the only likeness in metal

lifetimeextantBicon-medal-1595
1595 (dated on the medal)
The one portrait of Tycho in metal, and the only one that carries his motto as its whole reverse design. It exists in two variants — a larger of about 39 mm inscribed with the verse 'Arma, genus, fundi pereunt; durabile virtus et doctrina decus nobilitatis habent' (arms, lineage and estates perish; virtue and learning hold nobility's lasting honour), and a smaller of about 35 mm inscribed simply 'ESSE POTIVS QVAM HABERI', to be rather than to seem. Both show a bearded bust in a ruff, wearing the Order of the Elephant on a double chain, on the obverse, and the Brahe arms with the date on the reverse. The maker is unknown, and Mortensen noted that the portrait matches none of the established painted or engraved types — it may descend from a likeness now lost. His verdict, though, is undefended — no comparison is shown — and the medal's costume, the Elephant on its double chain and nothing else, matches his own description of the pre-1590 lineage of the Clementinum painting and the woodcut (W.1376): a candidate derivation that has never been tested. The reference usually given is Galster 53: Galster 1936, p. 40, covers the medals per Mortensen's citation, but whether no. 53 spans both variants is unverified, and Friis sources the two variants to two different national corpora — a Danish corpus for the smaller, the 1852 Bohemian-medals catalogue alone for the larger. Specimens are recorded in the Royal Collection of Coins and Medals in Copenhagen (Beckett saw two there, one silver and one bronze, with slightly different portraits), and Friis placed the fine larger specimen — probably silver, gilt, apparently chased — in the k.k. Mønt- og Medaillesamling in Vienna; the 'one at Prague' of the older literature is a citation ghost, resting on a page of Friis that names only the Prague-published catalogue of 1852 describing the larger variant, not any Prague holding. And Mortensen's caution matters — what survives in public cabinets is, as a rule, aftercasts rather than original strikings, and a specimen sold at auction in 2022 was a cast gilt electrotype.
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
Two variants exist — a larger of c. 39 mm with the verse legend and a smaller of c. 35 mm with the motto. Whether specimens were struck in Tycho's lifetime is a separate and softer question from whether the design is his: Friis says the Copenhagen piece 'synes at være støbt' — appears to be cast from a pierced example — and Mortensen generalises that public cabinets hold these medals 'i Reglen kun i Afstøbninger', usually only as casts. Against this stands a counter-witness: the Danske Magazin compilers of 1746 state that they personally inspected two 'Originaler' — one silver in the Royal Coin Cabinet, one base-metal in Klevenfeldt's collection — and describe specimens as 'afstemplede', struck. That is a live, unresolved conflict between primary accounts 125 years apart. The Nationalmuseet-photographed specimen (the smaller variant) shows no visible piercing at the rim.
Dated 1595 → 1595
The medal dates itself: the obverse legend reads 'EFFIGIES TYCHONIS BRAHE O F ... AETATIS 49' and the reverse carries the date 1595 beneath the Brahe arms — Tycho was 48 turning 49 that year. Whether it was struck in his lifetime is a separate and softer question, and the hedges have three distinct roots: Dreyer's 'it has been suggested' derives from — and truncates — Danske Magazin II (1746), pp. 166–168, whose editors offered an explicitly unprovable twin conjecture (the completion of the star catalogue and the great brass globe reaching usable form in 1595); Beckett's suggestion that Tycho gave the medal as a princely favour is independent of that, from a 1946 private letter to Mortensen, not derived from the 1746 discussion; and the 1746 editors also floated Tycho's nephew Tage Ottesen Tott, then 15–16, as a possible commissioner. No contemporary document of 1594–97 attesting the medal has been found in the material searched. So the object and its date are documented; the lifetime striking is a reasonable inference, not a documented fact.
Whereabouts

Attested at Copenhagen since the 18th century; no live record-level catalogue entry has been reached.

HolderDen kgl. Mønt- og Medaillesamling, Nationalmuseet, Copenhagen
Evidenceattested attested — not confirmed by a live record-level page
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

Copenhagen holds the silver smaller variant and an old brass/bronze copy of the larger — Friis and Beckett (p. 116) convergent. The fine larger specimen was placed by Friis (Bidrag til dansk Kunsthistorie, printed p. 232) in the k.k. Mønt- og Medaillesamling, Vienna — probably silver, gilt, apparently chased. Dreyer's 'collection at Prague' (1890, p. 228 n. 1) is a citation ghost: his cited page, Friis 1871 p. 363 n. 126, names only the Prague-published 1852 Bohemian-medals catalogue (Beschreibung der bisher bekannten böhmischen Privatmünzen und Medaillen, p. 16, Tab. III no. 17) and states no location. No live record-level page has been reached at either institution. Nationalmuseet photographed its smaller-variant specimen for Tycho Brahes Verden (2006), fig. 3, printed p. 89.

Open questions 5
  1. Georg Galster, Danske og norske Medailler og Jetons (Copenhagen 1936), no. 53, p. 40, which carries an illustration, remains unread; it is not digitised.
  2. The Copenhagen specimen's inventory number, and whether it is a strike or an aftercast — known only to Den kgl. Mønt- og Medaillesamling.
  3. The 1852 Beschreibung der bisher bekannten böhmischen Privatmünzen und Medaillen (Prague, 1852), p. 16, Tab. III no. 17 — the last unseen primary source in the Prague/Vienna chain and the source of the rejected 'Opus Factum' reading of the obverse's O F.
  4. Whether the KHM Münzkabinett, Vienna, still holds the ex-k.k. larger specimen.
  5. Whether the reverse distich 'Arma, genus, fundi pereunt …' has a neo-Latin antecedent — unchecked, and prior to any claim about its authorship.

16

The aetatis-50 portrait (Skokloster / Edinburgh) — the exile likeness

lifetimeextantBicon-standing-type
1597 (type; surviving canvases undated)
The last new likeness type made in Tycho's lifetime, and the one that shows him as he left Denmark: half-length, bareheaded and balding at fifty, in a black gold-buttoned doublet with an immense lace-edged ruff, a broad ornamented gold belt, rings on both hands and a pair of gloves held in the left. Two gold chains carry the enamelled Order of the Elephant on his breast. In the upper left corner an emblem panel shows a cone with a ball finial on a stepped pedestal, under a draped canopy suspended by cords from a hand emerging from cloud, flanked by winged putti; on the Skokloster canvas its inscribed band reads STANS TEGOR IN SOLIDO VENT[·] STREPAT IGNIS ET VNDA. The gold inscription fills the upper right. It is independent of the 1586 Gemperlin likeness family — a fresh sitting, from a man a decade older than the face the engravings kept reprinting. Versions are at Skokloster Castle in Sweden (the one shown here) and at the Royal Observatory Edinburgh in the Crawford collection, with a further version in the Nationalmuseum-administered portrait collection at Gripsholm Castle (NMGrh 1580). The leading account of the sitting is production in the Wandsbek exile itself, in 1597 — the place and year named by the dated inscription of the lost Copenhagen exemplar; the two painter entries in Tycho's own diary — the unexplained painter working on Hven from 6 March to 23 April 1591, and the 'Pictor' of 4 May 1596 — remain as residual alternatives for an earlier underlying likeness.
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
Original lost. Every located painting of this type is a copy, and the best of them are copies of a lost picture rather than of one another. Ståhlbrand 1946 (p. 133): of contemporary painted portraits made in Tycho's lifetime 'quite certainly none are preserved to our day'; and (p. 134) 'the original is quite certainly destroyed, but several replicas exist. The best copy is the one at Skokloster.' Håkansson (ed.) 2006, p. 133, agrees that the Skokloster canvas is 'probably a copy of a contemporary painting that has been lost'. Mortensen 1946 catalogues seven paintings of the type and calls every one of them a copy.
Dated 14 December 1596 → 1597
The dating rests on two different texts, not one. The surviving Skokloster and Edinburgh canvases carry a third-person caption — 'ÆTATIS SVÆ ANNO 50 COMPLETO … QVO POST DIVTINVM IN PATRIA EXILIVM LIBERTATI DESIDERATÆ DIVINO PROVISV RESTITVTVS EST' — with no place and no year, verified by direct examination of high-resolution photographs of both canvases and by SHM's own catalogue transcription. The dated text is attested only on the lost Copenhagen picture, via Resen, Inscriptiones Hafnienses (1668), pp. 335–336: first person ('restitutus fui'), placed and dated VANDESBECHI AN. MDXCVII, and signed TYCHO BRAHE OT. That exemplar's year is settled at 1597 — Resen's apostrophus numeral; Dreyer 1890, p. 264, verified against the printed page image; TBOO IX.211, sourced in its own apparatus to Cod. Vindob. lat. 10686; DSL's '1598' is a transcription slip in the modern digital edition. The lower bound of 14 December 1596, the day Tycho completed his fiftieth year, follows from 'aetatis suae anno 50 completo' and stands; but the 1597 anchor transfers to the surviving canvases by type-identity inference only, since their own captions are undated.
Whereabouts

No original survives. Seven copies are catalogued, six located and one lost; the best is at Skokloster.

HolderSkoklosters slott (Statens historiska museer), Håbo, Uppsala län, Sweden
Inventory11593_SKO
Evidencelocated located (live SHM record with digitised photograph) for the Skokloster copy; the type's original is lost
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

Skokloster's own catalogue states 'Originalet finns på observatoriet i Edinburg' — the original is at the Edinburgh observatory. That is superseded: Mortensen examined the Edinburgh canvas in person and reported it an old, partly restored copy.

Samuel Crompton exhibited the canvas at the Manchester Literary and Philosophical Society in October 1876 (Proceedings XVI, 1876–77, pp. 18–21; reprinted Memoirs 3rd ser. 6, 1879, pp. 77–81), had a Woodburytype reproduction made in 1878, and sold it at Capes, Dunn & Pilcher, Manchester, 4 October 1881, for £40; the Earl of Crawford bought it and presented it to the Observatory in 1888 with his astronomical library. Pre-1876 provenance is completely untraced. Dreyer used it as his 1890 frontispiece and wrote 'this portrait (or a copy of it)'. Crawford himself assumed his purchase was an original.

Open questions 8
  1. The Edinburgh band: FREMAT (Crompton's explicitly conjectural 1876 reconstruction) or STREPAT (as on Skokloster). It has never been read with certainty; conservation-grade imaging from the Royal Observatory Edinburgh, or examination in person, would settle it.

  3. TBOO IX.211's numeral has not been verified against the printed page image — the last open thread under the 1597 year claim.

17

The painted portraits in the finest Mechanica copies — Tycho between Ptolemy and Copernicus

lifetimeextantBicon-mechanica-watercolours
1598 – c. 1600
The grandest copies of the Mechanica (Wandsbek 1598) carry, on the leaf facing the title, a hand-painted watercolour portrait made individually for that copy — for the three dedicated exemplars (Copenhagen, Strahov, Weimar) demonstrably the last portrait campaign of Tycho's life, and the only one where each impression is unique; for the undedicated pair (Stockholm KVA and Kalocsa), which carry neither dedicatee nor dispatch date, a post-1601 completion by the heirs or Tengnagel cannot be formally excluded, though no post-death dispatch of any Mechanica copy is documented. The composition is a claim as much as a likeness. The bust sits in a blue oval; two winged putti above hold a cartouche lettered 'Effigies Tychonis Brahe'; below, two bearded figures in gold, lettered on their plinths COPERNICVS (left) and PTOLOMEVS (right), each lean on a gilt armillary sphere and gesture toward the arms roundel between them, around which runs Tycho's motto 'Esse potius quam haberi' — the astronomer placing himself, literally, between the two authorities his own system was built to supersede. He wears a fur-lined black gown over a gold brocade doublet with a heavy linked chain, a black belt, and holds a small dark object in his left hand. Despite Friis's and Mortensen's readings of an Elephant device on the chain, the full-resolution image shows no figurative pendant — the chain ends in a plain knot. Four exemplars are recorded by Mortensen: the Royal Library in Copenhagen, the Royal Library in Stockholm, the Strahov monastery in Prague, and the cathedral library at Kalocsa in Hungary. The Copenhagen exemplar is identified here as Det Kongelige Bibliotek, LN 432 2°, eks. 2 — Tycho's own presentation copy to Petr Vok z Rožmberka (Peter Wok von Rosenberg), lord of Krumlov and Třeboň, which carries in one volume its silk presentation binding stamped with Tycho's gilt portrait supralibros, Rožmberk's engraved ex-libris of 1609, Tycho's autograph dedication ('Dono Dat', signed Tycho Brahe and dated 'Anno 1600 Septembris Die 18'), and the watercolour on the leaf facing the title. Nationalmuseet reproduced this leaf in Tycho Brahes Verden (2006), fig. 8, credited to the Royal Library.
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
Each watercolour is unique — hand-painted individually for its copy, so every one is an original, and no two are quite alike. Vellev's claim that they 'must all derive from the same model' is refuted for at least one leaf: a first-hand inspection of the Weimar leaf found it a replica of the de Gheyn 1590 engraving, captioned aetatis 40 / anno 1586 — a demonstrably different and much earlier model.
Dated January 1598 (production of the Mechanica) → 24 October 1601
The Mechanica was produced from January 1598, which sets the campaign's lower bound; Tycho's death on 24 October 1601 caps it, and the bulk of the presentation copies had gone out by about 1600. The Copenhagen copy's autograph dedication of 18 September 1600 is that exemplar's terminus ante quem. The former lower-bound basis — an 'Aet: LII' age inscription on the watercolours — is withdrawn: full-resolution inspection of the Copenhagen leaf (July 2026) found no age inscription of any kind, Mortensen's actual printed reading is 'Aet: LI', type-generic and anchored to Andersen's 1886 etching rather than to any leaf, and Vellev's 'LII' remains unlocated.
Whereabouts

At least five exemplars are known; the Copenhagen one is fully digitised and public.

HolderDet Kongelige Bibliotek, Copenhagen
InventoryLN 432 2°, eks. 2
Evidencelocated located (live catalogue record plus a complete public IIIF surrogate at 2643 × 3609 px per leaf)

The dedication is in an accompanying autograph letter rather than written into the book — a real difference from the Copenhagen copy, where it is on the flyleaf. Von Biela, writing from Prague on 20 June 1824, gives the earliest first-hand description of the portrait: bald head, blue eyes, a long reddish beard, and a nose that does not look natural. He also transcribes the dedication: 'Illustri ac Generoso Domino, Dno. Johanni liber Baro ab Hasenburg, Capitaneo, S. Cæsar. Majestatis a Consiliis, Domino et amico suo in primis honorando Tycho Brahe'. Von Biela's letter was published in Astronomische Nachrichten III (1825), p. 256. Friis footnotes this portrait — the Strahov Mechanica one — to a reproduction in Danmarks Riges Historie IV (1897), p. 43.

Where the sources disagree 3
QuestionPositionsStatus
Whose dedication does the Strahov copy carry? — resolvedThis edition formerly said, citing Friis, that the Strahov copy carries Tycho's dedication to Peter Vok von Rosenberg. Friis says no such thing. He describes the Copenhagen copy first — 'I det store kongelige Bibliothek i Kjøbenhavn findes et Exemplar af Bogen, i hvilket der paa Bagsiden af Titelbladet er anbragt et med Vandfarve malet Portræt' — and then: 'I dette Exemplar af Bogen findes en af Tyge Brahe selv underskreven Dedikation til den bøhmiske Adelsmand Peter Vok von Rosenberg.' He then turns to 'et andet Exemplar … i Præmonstratenserklostret Strahof i Prag', whose autograph dedication is to Johann Liber Baro ab Hasenburg. Studnička 1901 (pp. 44–48) independently gives the Strahov copy as a presentation copy to Baron Johann von Hasenburg, in an accompanying autograph letter, in a green silk binding with the portrait supralibros on the front and the arms on the back.Settled, and the error was this edition's, not Friis's. Copenhagen = Peter Vok z Rožmberka; Strahov = Jan Zbyněk Zajíc z Házmburka. Friis had it right in the 1890s, and his account independently corroborates the object-level reading of KB's own digitisation.
'Royal Library in Stockholm' or Kungliga Vetenskapsakademien?Mortensen's list says the former; the Mechanica census records the latter (H1, the Wargentin copy).Resolved — same object. Mortensen's own later gloss ('Tycho Brahe Minder i Prag', p. 16) describes the Stockholm exemplar as belonging to the 'Sv. Vetenskapsakademien'; Norlind's exhaustive census records exactly one Stockholm Mechanica (KVA nr 1, Wargentin). 'Kgl. Bibliotek i Stockholm' was a loose institutional label.
How many exemplars?Four — Mortensen. At least five once Weimar is added. 'Three survive' also circulates, from Hasselberg via videnskab.dk.the defensible figure is at least five; 'three' should not be repeated
Settled
  1. The age inscription — resolved for Copenhagen: no age inscription exists on the leaf (full-resolution IIIF inspection, July 2026). Residual, still open: where Mortensen's 'Aet: LI' and Vellev's 'LII' actually sit — on Andersen's 1886 etching (Westergaard 1451), or on the Strahov, KVA or Kalocsa leaf, none inspected at reading resolution.
Open questions 2
  1. Where do Mortensen's 'Aet: LI' and Vellev's 'LII' actually sit — on Andersen's 1886 etching (Westergaard 1451), or on the Strahov, KVA or Kalocsa leaf? None has been inspected at reading resolution.
  2. Strahov holds a third Tycho watercolour, and it is of a different type — see the full-figure record. Whether the Strahov Mechanica's own watercolour matches the Copenhagen composition is now partially checked: the Studnička 1901 plate (p. 47) shows the Strahov leaf as a bust in an oval with an architectural/strapwork surround, two small flanking figures, a cartouche-like element above, and an arms roundel below — structurally cognate with Copenhagen, but the photogravure cannot confirm lettering or figure identities. A modern image from Strahov is still needed.

Elsewhere in Tychoniana Mechanica (1598)

18

The Týn church epitaph, Prague — the effigy that closes the series

lifetimeextantBicon-teyn-epitaph
c. 1604 or some years after
The memorial relief in the Church of Our Lady before Týn: Tycho carved life-size in red Slivenec marble, 200 × 115 cm, standing in a suit of armour already old-fashioned in his own day, his right hand resting on a globe set upon the family arms and his left on his sword hilt, a plumed helmet at his feet. The prosthetic nose is plainly shown. The carving is coarse, and the workshop is unattributed — Mortensen (1946, without citation) called it Austrian, by type-resemblance to the Berka von Dauba monument in the same church (a comparandum itself unverified); Vellev (2006, equally uncited) takes it for a local Prague stonemason's workshop responsible for a series of monuments in Prague churches. It was ground down and restored for the tercentenary in 1901, when the floor slab lettered simply TYCHO BRAHE was also laid. Casts are at Frederiksborg (inv. A 155, maker 'Steffensen', 1885–86) and in the Round Tower's historical collection in Copenhagen (a life-size plaster made c. 1867 from the original by F. R. Friis, transferred from the Nationalmuseet) — both pre-dating the 1901 restoration. It is the last image of Tycho made from living memory, and the point where the lifetime iconography hands over to the printed afterlife.
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
An original carved monument, made in Prague by people who had seen him — but set up after his death, and heavily restored in 1901.
Dated 1604 (Kirstine's death — the only sourced terminus post quem for the erection; the 1601 burial dates the grave, not the monument) → after 1604 (no sourced completion date)
Tycho died in Prague on 24 October 1601 and was buried in the Týn church, but the monument's erection is sourced only as after his widow Kirstine's death in 1604 (Friis 1871 p. 298, Mortensen 1946 p. 74, Norlind 1970 p. 318); no source attests an earlier figure-stone phase. It therefore stands past the lifetime boundary — but it was made in Prague by people who had seen him, and it closes the series.
Whereabouts

In situ in the Church of Our Lady before Týn, Prague, against a pier near the chancel.

HolderKostel Panny Marie před Týnem, Staroměstské náměstí, Prague 1
Evidencelocated located (architecturally fixed; the 2010 reinterment campaign necessarily handled it)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

Red Slivenec marble, 200 × 115 cm (Mortensen 1942 gives 2.03 × 1.15 m). Figure-stone and inscription plate are physically distinct elements (Vellev 2006), but no source dates them separately: Friis 1871 and Norlind 1970 describe a single erection by the heirs after Kirstine's death in 1604, and a two-campaign history is this edition's hypothesis, unattested. The epitaph text was composed by Jakob Typotius. The death year was miscut MDCIII for MDCI and patched with coloured filler (Vellev 2006, p. 230); the upper plate gives 29 October against the frame's XXIV OCTOB (Vellev 2006, p. 232). Four-ancestor arms Brahe/Bille/Rud/Ulfstand, with only two helmets where four belong (Vellev 2006, p. 230) — on Vellev's explanation possibly deliberate economy, since all four families sometimes used the same buffalo-horn-and-peacock-feather crest. Restored in 1901 by Jan Herain for the tercentenary: surfaces cleaned and smoothed, the ground painted white, cornices repaired, letters blackened, and the missing top ornament replaced by a small obelisk; Herain's measurements and notes of the work were no longer to be found as of 2006 (Vellev p. 235). Of the two Copenhagen casts, the identification of Frederiksborg A 155 as the Týn figure is well-supported inference — structured record, catalogue photograph, independent 1937 corroboration — not a documentary source naming both together.

Danske Magazin II (Copenhagen, 1746), p. 353copper engraving, printed on the text pagethe earliest printed record of the monument, and the witness to its pre-1901 state
Attributed to Johann Martin Bernigeroth by the 2006 volume; the plate as printed carries no engraver's signature legible at 600 dpi and the 1746 text names none, so the attribution is not hardened here. The 1746 text documents the production chain: drawn in 1743 in Prague for Tycho de Hofman, the Danish Chancery secretary there for Maria Theresa's Bohemian coronation, and engraved for his Portraits historiques (The Hague, 1746), the plate then lent to Danske Magazin (bind 2, pp. 356–57) — naming the commissioner but no engraver. The Bernigeroth attribution traces to Vellev's fig.-3 caption alone, and his 'efter Danske Magazin' could even describe a later derivative. A collation trap: it is printed on the text page at the head of p. 353, not on an inserted leaf.
Grinder-Hansen ed. 2006, fig. 3; autopsy of the 1746 volume

The image programme

19

The Brahe coat-of-arms supralibros

programmeextantBicon-arms-supralibros
1588–1601
Tycho's armorial stamp, impressed in gold as the lower-board partner of the portrait oval: on every binding in the attested corpus where both boards have actually been examined — presentation copies and own-library bindings alike — the two ovals travel together as a matched die-pair (Kleinschnitzová states outright that Tycho used the pair on both classes; Prandtl's 1932 frontispiece shows the UT Austin Gesner with gilt ovals on both boards; Mosley photographs both covers of a presentation Epistolae). The device is the plain undifferenced Brahe paternal shield — sable, a pale argent — under helm, coronet and a tall panache crest, not an ancestral marshalling (Grinder-Hansen; Vellev). It is ringed by a motto in an oval medallion ('Arma, genus, fundi pereunt, durabile virtus / et doctrina decus nobilitatis habent' — 'arms, lineage, estates perish; virtue and learning hold nobility's lasting honour'); per Dreyer's apparatus in TBOO IX, 175, the couplet was printed by Tycho's own press at Uraniborg in 1584 — some four years before the die's earliest dated impression — so the stamp adopted an existing text, and the same distich recurs on the reverse of the larger (39 mm) 1595 medal. The familiar claim that the arms occur 'in the same two sizes as the portrait stamp' rests on Mortensen's single joint clause for portrait-and-arms together (7.6 × 5.8 / 5.8–5.9 × 4.3 cm, internally inconsistent between his 1931 and 1966 articles); he never measured the arms oval separately, and the first independent measurement — the Stockholm KB De mundi, ≈6.6 × 5.3 cm from the KB scan on a transferred scale — fits neither figure. One first-hand witness does speak to the question, and it is much the earliest: Danske Magazin II (1746), p. 322, describing the Royal Library's blue-satin Mechanica (LN 432 2°, eks. 2) board by board, reports the arms die on the lower cover as 'i selv samme Form og Størrelse' as the portrait die on the upper — same shape, same size, on one volume, seen by the compilers themselves. That is a statement about a single binding rather than about the dies in general, but it is evidence of a kind Mortensen's joint clause never had. The same page prints the couplet in a variant reading — 'Arma, Genus, Fundi pereunt: Durabile Virtus / Et Doctrinæ decus Nobilitatis habent', with DOCTRINÆ in the genitive against the nominative doctrina given above; the variant is recorded, not adopted, since the nominative rests on Dreyer's TBOO IX apparatus for the 1584 Uraniborg printing.
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
An original stamp, surviving only as impressions; the die is untraced, as with the portrait die.
Whereabouts

Impressions survive on the back covers of Tycho's presentation and library bindings.

HolderDocumented on the Strahov Mechanica (arms on the back, portrait on the front of the same volume), Prague
Evidenceedition multiple extant as impressions; the die untraced
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

Also on Wolfenbüttel HAB Astronomica 8 and the Smithsonian/Dibner Epistolae.

Copy-level carriers

Arms supralibros documented on: Stockholm KB De mundi RAR: 106 A (arms on the lower cover, verified first-hand from the KB scan, p. 493); Görlitz De mundi (dedication copy to Bartholomäus Scultetus, 8 May 1588, per Norlind p. 127 — the earliest dated arms attestation); Smithsonian/Dibner Epistolae (arms supralibros on covers). The Wolfenbüttel Epistolae HAB Astronomica 8 line (arms + motto on back cover, behind the portrait front) is unreconciled — a July 2026 search returned nothing for Wolfenbüttel; a direct query to the Herzog August Bibliothek is pending. The Bodleian Savile De mundi is a documented negative (no supralibros on either cover). Danske Magazin II (1746), p. 322 supplies the earliest attestation of all, and one not previously in this list: the arms die on the lower cover of the Copenhagen KB Mechanica LN 432 2°, eks. 2 (the Petr Vok z Rožmberka presentation copy), described first-hand as the same-size partner of the portrait oval on the upper cover.

The inserted author portrait in Epistolae and Mechanica copies

programmeextantBicon-mechanica-author-portrait
1596–1598
The engraved portrait was inserted loose — often hand-coloured — in copies of the Epistolae (1596) and in presentation copies of the Mechanica (1598): the interior complement to the gilt portrait on the cover. For the Epistolae, printed at Uraniborg, the cause is documented — Tycho's press had no intaglio capability. The Mechanica was printed at Wandsbek by Philipp von Ohr, a printer styled Chalcographus, whose shop supplied four integral copperplates for that very book; there the operative constraint was custody of the de Gheyn plate, not the absence of a press. Practice varied copy by copy — engraved impressions, watercolour substitutes, and copies with no portrait at all are documented. The Smithsonian/Dibner Epistolae carries a hand-coloured impression of the second engraving, identified by direct examination (2026).
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
Original engraved impressions, inserted loose — often hand-coloured: at Uraniborg (Epistolae, 1596) because the press had no intaglio capability; at Wandsbek (Mechanica, 1598) despite the printer's copperplate capacity — the constraint there was custody of the de Gheyn plate.
Whereabouts

Copy-level: engraved impression, watercolour substitute, or no portrait at all — what a given volume carries varies precisely because insertion was loose.

Evidenceedition multiple extant as impressions inside surviving copies
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

The Smithsonian/Dibner Epistolae impression was identified as the second engraving (v2) by marker examination of the full-resolution digitisation (July 2026); no institutional catalogue states the plate, and the insertion date into that volume is undetermined (earliest documented owner: Uffenbach).

21

The small gilt brass quadrant — an iconographic instrument

programmelostBicon-gilt-quadrant
c. 1573
A small gilt brass quadrant carrying a deliberate iconographic programme — Nonnian subdivisions (Pedro Nunes's nested-arc method of graduation, which Tycho himself found insufficient in practice) and 'an emblem of life and death' — rather than serving only as a measuring tool; a point where the Instruments and Iconography pillars meet, the instrument as image. Built c. 1573 in Denmark: Thoren (Lord of Uraniborg, p. 75 n. 5) anchors the date on Tycho's own log of the lunar eclipse of 8 December 1573 (TBOO X, 38–39), which he reports contains an unambiguous reference to this instrument. Christianson's caption (On Tycho's Island, p. 257, Fig. 51) — 'probably built c.1568–69 with Hugo Blotius in Basel' — does depict this very instrument, but the date carries no citation, contradicts his own adjoining prose, and is dropped in his 2020 Measure of the Heavens; it is retained here as recorded dissent. The quadrant opens the Mechanica (1598) on an engraved (intaglio) plate, where most of the book's images are woodcuts. Its motto reads VIVIMVS INGENIO, CÆTERA MORTIS ERVNT — first-person plural, against the impersonal 'Vivitur ingenio' of the adage tradition — and the emblem's description is independently attested in the 1591 instrument synopsis for Landgrave Wilhelm IV (TBOO VI, 284–285, item XXV) and in Resen 1668 (pp. 344–345, the same emblem executed on the Stjerneborg wall); these witnesses fix the description by 1591 but do not date the instrument's construction. The subject of a dedicated iconographic study (Perkins & Taub 2015, now read in full); the one earlier study, Segonds 1994, is cited but remains unread.
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
The instrument is destroyed, and the emblem it carried is not a portrait of Tycho. Tycho's own description (Opera Omnia V, p. 14) calls the figure 'Juvenis laurea insignitus' — a laurel-crowned young man holding a celestial globe and a book. Neither Tycho, nor Perkins & Taub 2015, nor the 2006 book identifies him as Tycho. The record is kept because the emblem belongs to Tycho's self-presentation programme, not because it depicts him.
Whereabouts

Untraced after the Hven era. The Prague-destruction account is collective (Dreyer, hedged) and item-level documentation is absent from the 1607–1650 Prague inventories; the instrument was obsolete by c.1580 and its presence in the 1600 Prague shipment is unconfirmed.

Evidenceuntraced untraced
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

Dreyer 1890 pp. 365–66: 'At the present day there is neither at Prague nor at Copenhagen the smallest vestige of Tycho's celebrated instruments' — his own hedge 'are said to have' on the 1619 destruction is preserved. Only the great brass globe survived, and it burned at Copenhagen in October 1728. The Detmold Mechanica (Lippische Landesbibliothek Nl 70.4°) is the only public deep-zoom surrogate of the plate (IIIF manifest digitale-sammlungen.llb-detmold.de/i3f/v20/2547750/manifest, plate at canvas 2547769), but its emblem roundel is uncoloured line-work within an otherwise richly hand-coloured plate; the red/green emblem colouring is known only from BL C.45.h.3 (Perkins & Taub Fig. 3), which has no digital surrogate.

22

The printed views of Uraniborg and Stjerneborg

programmeextantAicon-uraniborg-views
1585–1598
Tycho controlled the image of his establishment as carefully as his own likeness. The earliest graphic representation is the Orthographia broadsheet — three woodcuts of Uraniborg in elevation, bird's-eye view and ground-plan, printed by early 1585 on Nielsen's own hedged inference — one print with several attested distributions (to Rantzau in 1585, to Wilhelm IV in 1586, to Blotius in 1592), surviving in the exemplar sent on 1 March 1586 to Wilhelm IV (Hessisches Staatsarchiv Marburg, Best. 3 II, Nr. 1070) and retrieved on loan by Eiler Nystrom in 1912. How the Uraniborg view in Braun & Hogenberg's Civitates orbis terrarum descends from it is a point on which the two 1921 authorities disagree: Nielsen (Bogtrykkeri paa Hveen) holds the Braun plate a direct copy of the Marburg sheet, while Beckett & Christensen derive it from the lost 1585 woodcut sent to Hajek, the Vienna pen-drawing being a collateral witness and explicitly not the direct model. The Braun & Hogenberg plate (engraved Cologne 1586; published 1588 in the atlas's fourth volume) added a map of Hven and the 'Apotheosis of the Sound' (Christianson, figs. 21–22). The Mechanica (1598) carries the celebrated plates of Uraniborg, Stjerneborg and their instruments, but adds only one new architectural image — the east facade, a copperplate — and otherwise reprints the 1596 Epistolae blocks, so that 1598 dates the printing, not the image, for the reused views. Through these printed views the long-demolished buildings are still known. Beckett & Christensen's 1921 study established the reliability of the sequence: the Marburg broadsheet is the most reliable, carrying details found nowhere else; the Epistolae (1596) and Mechanica (1598) views are progressively prettified but less accurate; and the Epistolae map of Hven derives from a drawing by Willem Blaeu, Tycho's pupil. Measured against Charlier's 1901 excavation, all of them are unreliable in architectural detail — corrected by Charles Christensen's measured reconstruction (see Loca).
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
Original printed views, controlled by Tycho as carefully as his own likeness. Edition multiples.
Whereabouts

Survive wherever the books survive; the plate reproduced here is from the Royal Danish Library's copy.

Evidencelocated located (KB's digitised hand-coloured exemplar)

23

Tycho's portrait on the celestial globes — a distribution channel begun in his lifetime

programmeextantAicon-blaeu-globes
from 1594; Blaeu's Tychonic globes from 1597/98, portrait confirmed on extant specimens from 1603
A miniature engraved portrait of Tycho set into the printed gores of celestial globes — on the 1594 van Langren globe, a fully contemporary likeness with its own cartouche apparatus, read at object level in 2026 from Mosley's fig. 4.7: an age tablet ÆTATIS 47 (Tycho was 47 from 14 December 1593 to 13 December 1594 — the caption was computed fresh for the globe, where every de Gheyn print freezes age 40/1586), his motto NON HABERI SED ESSE compressed into one crowning tablet, BRAHER/BILLER banderoles in the print's own orthography, an Effigies tablet using Tycho's own foreign name for Hven ('in insula Scarlatina HVÆNA', an epithet whose only pre-1594 printed source is his own De mundi preface), a scroll counting some 800 stars from his observations plus some 200 southern stars from the ancients, and the privilege formula 'studio & consensu Nobilis Viri TYCHONIS BRAHE' — a composite not assemblable from a print alone, i.e. on-object evidence of a Tychonic information channel. The mechanism claim stays part documented, part inferred: Mosley's 'must have given' each maker a portrait impression remains his own uncited inference; what is documented is the sanction (the on-globe privilege citation, and Blaeu's six months at Uraniborg 1595–96). The miniature itself is a laterally reversed bust; a 2026 close read leans it toward the second de Gheyn design (beret worn, no Frederik II medal, single doubled chain, turned-down collar), which the corrected v2-design window (February–March 1592 to ≤5 September 1595) admits for 1594 — the earlier claim that it 'must descend from the 1590 engraving' rested on misreading v2's terminus ante quem as a post quem and is withdrawn; but a second reading, made against the first, held the second-engraving identification at medium confidence (a v1 reduced to a bust-in-oval, shedding parapet, medal and chains to miniaturization, is not excluded at halftone resolution — first-generation photography of the Frankfurt globe is the gate). The globes carried his face across Europe on somebody else's product, at no cost to him — and the convention propagated beyond any authorial supply: the Veen/Hondius globe of 1613 carries the portrait with the legend 'Effigies Nob. viri Tychonis Brahe... Aetatis 47' (Stevenson's transcription, verified) — the frozen age-47 pointing back to a 1593/94 model at a date when Tycho could not have furnished anything.
Maker
Jacob Floris van Langren and his sons Arnold (who cut the lettering) and Hendrik (the figures), per the globe's own imprint; then Willem Janszoon Blaeu (1571–1638).
Original or copy
Printed multiples on manufactured objects — originals of their kind. Not an afterlife phenomenon: the practice begins in Tycho's lifetime and at his own initiative.
Dated 1594 → after 1630 (latest located specimen, Museo Galileo 348); terminal edition undetermined
The practice begins with the van Langren globe of 1594 — the Tychonic (third) state of a 32.5-cm celestial line the family had issued since 1586 with pre-Tychonic positions (1586 Linköping; 1589 Greenwich GLB0099, positions probably Rudolf Snellius's; 1594 Frankfurt — Dekker, History of Cartography vol. 3, App. 6.1 nos. 130/137/148, verified verbatim, August 2026). Tycho's data and portrait were thus retrofitted onto an existing commercial product. Blaeu's own first celestial globe followed in 1597/98 (Dekker's census says 1597, Mosley p. 246 says 1598, citing the same van der Krogt pages; Warner 1971, the arbiter, unread) — it survives only as 9 of 12 gores at Houghton Library, Harvard (*51-2459 PF), and whether the portrait zone is among them is indeterminate. Earliest extant Blaeu specimen with confirmed portrait presence remains 1603. At the upper end, the latest securely located portrait-bearing specimen is the Joan Blaeu globe of after 1630; no source names a terminal portrait-bearing edition.
Whereabouts

One van Langren globe of the 1594 Tychonic state survives (Frankfurt); the 32.5-cm van Langren celestial line survives in three copies across its states (1586 Linköping, 1589 Greenwich, 1594 Frankfurt), the earlier two pre-Tychonic and portrait-less. Of Blaeu globes of 1603: Oxford (inv. 23709, portrait museum-confirmed) and Rome (Biblioteca Angelica); the Science Museum London specimen (1980-1913) is the same edition but its record is portrait-silent — catalogue silence being demonstrably non-diagnostic (RMG's granular record of its own 1603 Blaeu, GLB0101, likewise omits the portrait that edition carries). Later states after 1621 and after 1630 (the Metropolitan Museum's, portrait unconfirmed though printed from the 1603 plates; the Accorsi-Ometto's, asserted in a caption; the Museo Galileo's, confirmed).

HolderHistorisches Museum Frankfurt, Frankfurt am Main, Germany
InventoryHMF X 14609
Evidencelocated located (record-level Deutsche Digitale Bibliothek entry: Historisches Museum Frankfurt, celestial globe, van Langren family, 1594, inv. X 14 609)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

The sole survivor of the 1594 Tychonic state (Mosley pp. 231–232, figs. 4.6/4.7) — the specimen that carries the record's lower bound. Provenance: the Frankfurt Ratsbibliothek, whose librarian Johann Martin Waldschmidt (from 1691) expanded the library into instruments; the museum's blog says presumably bought at the Frankfurt Book Fair (medium confidence; Glasemann 1999 pp. 15–20, the museum's own catalogue, would settle it and is print-only). The latest securely located portrait-bearing specimen is the Joan Blaeu globe of after 1630 at the Museo Galileo, Florence, inv. 348 (diam. 680 mm, from the Medici collections; the catalogue notes 'There is a portrait of Tycho Brahe'). The Metropolitan Museum's Blaeu globe (1990.84, after 1621) is verified to exist and its 1990 acquisition bulletin states it prints from the 1603 plates — portrait presence therefore probable by plate identity but unobserved (both open-access photographs show the wrong hemispheres). The Oxford History of Science Museum's 1603 globe is inv. 23709, portrait confirmed by the museum. The 1613 portrait-bearing globe is RMG GLB0122 (535 mm), by Adriaen Veen with Jodocus Hondius Jr. On the Blaeu and Hondius-line globes the portrait sits in the southern hemisphere, below Cetus near Phoenix — the face museum photography systematically misses; on the 1594 van Langren globe it sits in the southern sky near Eridanus and Piscis Notius.

Where the sources disagree 1
QuestionPositionsStatus
Piety or marketing?Grinder-Hansen 2006, p. 245 (read at page level, August 2026): Blaeu 'producerede stjerneglober til det europæiske marked baseret på Tycho Brahes resultater — og pietetsfuldt forsynede med hans portræt' — 'pietetsfuldt' (piously) is his own word, though in that sentence he calls Blaeu only 'the Dutch cartographer who had been on Hven 1595–96', not a pupil. Mosley 2007 pp. 248–249 documents the opposite mechanism at the origin — the image reached the makers in Tycho's lifetime with his sanction, 'to grant legitimacy to the instruments'.Mosley governs the origin — lifetime, sanctioned, privilege-documented; Grinder-Hansen's piety reading remains defensible for the posthumous continuation, which the record's own latest specimens embody, and the 1613 Veen/Hondius globe shows the convention self-propagating after 1601.
Open questions 5
  1. First-generation photography of HMF X 14609, which only the Historisches Museum Frankfurt can supply: the single gate on the miniature's model (second-engraving design or a reduced first engraving, held at medium confidence), the unread right-hand cartouche, the States-General privilege term (ad decem read against Mosley's fifteen years), and pixel confirmation of ÆTATIS 47 on the object.
  2. Whether the Houghton gore fragment of Blaeu's first celestial globe (9 of 12 gores, *51-2459 PF) includes the portrait zone — Warner, Imago Mundi 25 (1971) 29–38 is the arbiter (paywalled), and the call number itself has no live catalogue record (a reference query to Houghton would close both).
  3. The Plancius–Hondius globe of 1597/98: Secondary sources put 'a portrait of Tycho below Cetus' in that globe's tradition, possibly by conflation with its 1602 Blaeu copy or the 1613 globe. Test object: the twelve gores found 1983 in the Nicolai Collection, Württembergische Landesbibliothek Stuttgart. If real, the lifetime portrait-globe census has two or three members, not one, and the 1613 Aetatis-47 legend may descend via the Hondius house.
  4. The date of Blaeu's 10-cm celestial globe (Mosley: 'in the intervening period', with a Tycho attribution) — the one live remaining lifetime-globe candidate; van der Krogt 1993 pp. 503–505.
  5. The month of the Hondius 1601 globe (Tycho died 24 October 1601) — its lifetime status is genuinely undetermined.
Relations
  • depicts Tycho Brahe
  • appears in Epistolae astronomicae (1596)
  • reconstructed derivation of De Gheyn's second engraving (Westergaard 1394) — the corrected portrait §6

The afterlife

9 records
24

The posthumous copies (1602 onward)

afterlifeextantAicon-posthumous-copies
1602–1655
After Tycho's death (October 1601) his engineered image kept propagating from the de Gheyn plates. The Nuremberg branch runs through two plates one generation apart: an unsigned same-direction copy of the second engraving, cut for Hülsius's press for the 1601 Epistolarum, and the 1602 Mechanica plate cut in turn from that copy — the two share the same idiosyncratic caption errors, with identical line-breaks. The first plate itself, in Prague by 1602, printed integrally in at least one examined Progymnasmata copy (Copenhagen), though two further copies examined at full resolution carry no portrait at all — a small or copy-specific press-run rather than the edition's norm. Impressions lettered 'Marco Sadeler excudit' are restrikes from the original copper, though the date of that address and the identity of the Sadeler behind it are unresolved. Jacob van Meurs re-cut the type half a century on for the 1655 Hague edition of Gassendi's biography. So far as traced, the century's engraved portrait copies descend from the two Amsterdam plates; the exceptions are the Rudolphine Tables frontispiece figure of 1627, engraved by Georg Celer (Cöler) of Nuremberg in a distinct engraved lineage whose ultimate model is undetermined, and two unattributed woodcuts whose models are likewise undetermined.
Maker
not recorded
Original or copy
The copy-stemma of the two engraved Amsterdam plates only; carved, painted, and instrument-borne likenesses are treated in their own records (icon-blaeu-globes, icon-console-clock-1642, icon-teyn-epitaph, icon-standing-type).
Whereabouts

Dispersed edition multiples across European collections.

Evidenceedition multiple extant as impressions and objects
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

The Nuremberg descent runs through two plates one generation apart: the unsigned copy-plate cut for Hülsius's press for the 1601 Epistolarum, and the 1602 Mechanica plate cut from it, repeating its caption errors with identical line-breaks.

Where the sources disagree 1
QuestionPositionsStatus
Does every seventeenth-century image descend from the two Amsterdam plates?The engraved gilt-brass side plate of a 1642 console clock (Nationalmuseet, inv. D138 — a number itself unverified: the museum portal's page finds the object but gives no date, a portal search returns nothing, and the number is absent from the 2006 book's p. 146) shows Tycho pointing at his own world system, and the 2006 book identifies its model as the copperplate in Kepler's Rudolphine Tables (1627) — a frontispiece engraved by Georg Celer (Cöler) of Nuremberg (Gattei p. 363; the engraver's sheet is GNM Nuremberg, inv. MP 2799). Whether the clock's face (as opposed to its pose) is de Gheyn-derived remains open on the borrowed-head/invented-body pattern; object-level detail is treated at icon-console-clock-1642.the universal claim was too strong as written. The Rudolphine lineage shows Tycho as proprietor of a world system rather than as a face; whether it is fully independent of de Gheyn or carries a de Gheyn-derived face on an invented body is undetermined — no visual collation exists either way
Open questions 7
  1. Per-copy portrait survey: the four Copenhagen (KB) Progymnasmata copies, the 1603/1610 issue-states, and surviving 1601 and 1610 Epistolarum witnesses — decisive for the titelauflage soft point (whether the 1610 reissue could have required a fresh portrait pull).
  2. Digitaler Portraitindex records 41–91 remain unexamined — the counterexample sweep is not closed.
  3. Thieme-Becker Band XXIX Sadeler entry (pp. 299–303) unopened — the one standard authority on the Sadelers not yet consulted.
  4. Date of the 'Marco Sadeler excudit' address on New Hollstein 238 State II — New Hollstein gives none.
  5. Portrait-leaf mechanics of the Epistolarum issues (integral title-verso versus inserted plate leaf) — decides how absolute the titelauflage reading of the 1610 reissue is.
  6. Models of the two unattributed LWL Münster woodcuts and of the Freher 1688 Theatrum book-portrait — undetermined.
  7. Whether the 1654 Paris first printing of Gassendi's Vita carries any portrait — the van Meurs plate is documented only in Vlacq's Hague 1655 second edition, and this record's 1655 end-date and icon-gassendi-frontispiece must agree on that basis.

The Gassendi Vita frontispiece (1655) — the portrait's afterlife

afterlifeextantAicon-gassendi-frontispiece
1654–1655
The engraved Tycho portrait of Gassendi's Tychonis Brahei Vita — bound before Book One of the 1654 Paris first edition, and moved to the frontispiece slot in the 1655 Hague edition: a bust of Tycho in a flat turned-down collar and a single doubled chain, without the Frederik II medal, within a triumphal arch hung with his sixteen ancestral coats-of-arms, captioned 'Effigies Tychonis Brahe Ottonidis Dani… ætatis suæ anno 40, anno Dni 1586', with his motto 'Non Haberi sed Esse' ('not to seem, but to be') in the lower corners. It copies de Gheyn's second engraving — established not by the caption or the sixteen quarterings, which are identical in both de Gheyn states, but by the dispositive discriminators: the tablet's HVENNA and INSTRVMENTORVMQVE ASTRONOMICORVM, the second state's RØNNOR and STORMVASE armorials, the plumed beret worn on the head, and the arms transposed as in the second state. It shows how Tycho's carefully self-fashioned image was reused, a half-century after his death, to authorise the first full biography.
Maker
Jacob van Meurs I (Amsterdam, c. 1619/20 – 1679), after Jacques de Gheyn II. The signature 'I.v.Meurs. Scul.' appears only in the state used in 1655; the 1654 state is unsigned, so the cutting — complete by 1654 — is attributed to van Meurs from the same-plate identity, while his signing of the 1655 state is documented.
Original or copy
A posthumous engraved copy after de Gheyn's second engraving (New Hollstein 239 copy b): in use unsigned before Book One of the 1654 Paris first edition, then signed by Jacob van Meurs I ('I.v.Meurs. Scul.') as frontispiece of the 1655 Hague edition.
Whereabouts

An edition multiple: found in copies of both the 1654 Paris first edition (unsigned, before Book One) and the 1655 Hague edition (signed, as frontispiece); no copy lacking it is documented, but no multi-copy collation has been made.

Evidenceedition multiple extant as impressions
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

Pierre Gassendi, Tychonis Brahei … Vita, 2nd ed. (The Hague: Vlacq, 1655).

Relations
  • depicts Tycho Brahe
  • appears in Gassendi, Tychonis Brahei vita (1654)
  • documented derivation of De Gheyn's second engraving (Westergaard 1394) — the corrected portrait §6

The Kågeröd epitaph, 1613 — Tycho kneeling among his parents' children

afterlifeextantBicon-kageroed-epitaph
1613
The large painted epitaph to Otte Brahe and Beate Bille in Kågeröd church, showing the parents with their ten children kneeling under the Crucifixion: five sons from the father (Tyge, Steen, Knud, Axel, Jørgen) and five daughters from the mother (Lisbet, Maren, Margrethe, Kirsten, Sophie). The daughters kneel in strict birth order; the sons do not — Knud, the youngest, is placed third. Tycho is the figure nearest his father, identified by a painted gold label, 'Lerde Tyge B.' — the learned Tyge Brahe.
Maker
Unrecorded; the Tycho head repainted between 1862 and c. 1895 — by Magnus Petersen according to Mortensen 1946 (unfootnoted and uncorroborated; Petersen is documented at the epitaph only as recorder of its pre-restoration state, Nationalmuseet inv. 572 / DMR-193014, 1860).
Original or copy
An original 1613 panel — but not an independent likeness. The loss of the Tycho face is attested by Horace Marryat, visiting in 1860 ('of Tycho's scarcely a vestige remains', publ. 1862), whose plea for restoration also fixes the panel as still unrestored at publication; Mortensen 1946, who cites Marryat in the same footnote and is therefore not an independent second witness, reports the head completely flaked and repainted after the Frederiksborg portrait, attributing the repaint to Magnus Petersen — an attribution that is unfootnoted and uncorroborated (see maker). The repaint falls between 1862 (hard, from Marryat) and c. 1895 (probable, from the post-restoration reproduction Mortensen cites in Danmarks Adels Aarbog XII, itself unexamined); its model was necessarily a COPY of the Frederiksborg original, which burned 16–17 December 1859 — plausibly C. A. Jensen's 1838 copy, Mortensen's own surrogate for the type. The face is therefore a 19th-century derivative of the Gemperlin type sitting on a 1613 panel, and has no evidential value as a likeness.
Dated 1613 → 1613
The tablet is dated by its own erection, twelve years after Tycho's death.
Whereabouts

In situ in Kågeröds kyrka, Skåne. Placed in front of the blocked south portal (former vapenhus entrance) in the 1914 Wåhlin renovation; reportedly moved to the sacristy in the 2022 interior renovation (Regionmuseet Skåne KrM 2022:40 — title confirmed, report unread).

HolderKågeröds kyrka (Kågeröd-Röstånga församling, Lunds stift), Kågeröd, Svalöv kommun, Skåne, Sweden
InventoryBebyggelseregistret anläggnings-ID 21300000008127 (church building; no object-level inventory number located; fastighet Svalöv Kågeröd 18:1)
Evidencelocated located (photographed in the church in 2021; handled during the 2020–22 restoration)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

The 2006 book's credit line 'Nationalmuseet, Roberto Fortuna foto' is a photographic credit only — the object is in Sweden and always has been. Note also that 'the fifth kneeling son' counts position from the left, the eldest son's place, not birth order.

Open questions 4
  1. The full text of Regionmuseet Skåne KrM 2022:40 and the 2011 Skånes Målerikonservatorer condition assessment — the untested paint stratigraphy; until one is read, the reported total loss of the 1613 face has never been checked against physical evidence.
  2. The Danmarks Adels Aarbog XII (1895) plate — whether it shows the repainted state, as Mortensen's characterisation implies but no one has verified, and whether it carries a draughtsman or restorer credit.
  3. Nationalmuseet DMR-193014 at full resolution (6075 × 7415 px, never visually inspected) — and whether it is identical with the 'Tegning' Mortensen cites: its medium field reads 'photograph'.
  4. Whether the gold label 'Lerde Tyge B.' is 1613 lettering or renewed.
Relations
  • depicts Tycho Brahe
  • documented derivation of The Frederiksborg oil (attributed to Gemperlin) — known through C. A. Jensen's 1838 copy §6
Sources
Mortensen, 'Portræter af Tycho Brahe' (Cassiopeia, 1946) — the portrait censusGrinder-Hansen (ed.), Tycho Brahes Verden (Nationalmuseet, 2006)Marryat, One Year in Sweden I (London, 1862) — the 1860 eyewitnessSimonsson, Kågeröd kyrka: kulturhistorisk karaktäristik och bedömning (2015)Nationalmuseet, Antikvarisk-Topografisk Arkiv, inv. 572 / DMR-193014 (1860)

The 1642 console clock — Tycho as proprietor of a world system

afterlifeextantBicon-console-clock-1642
1623 or 1642 (both unfooted)
An engraved and gilt brass side plate from a console clock, paired with a matching Copernicus plate: the two astronomers stand pointing at their respective world systems. A banderole reads HYPOTYPOSIS Systematis mundani a Tychone inventi, with the motto 'Quid si sic'. A century after Copernicus, the Tychonic system still had its adherents, and this is what that looked like as a domestic object. The matching plate reads HYPOTYPOSIS Systematis mundi Copernicani, with the motto 'Sic movetur Mundus'.
Maker
Unrecorded. The 2006 caption derives the Tycho figure from the Rudolphine Tables frontispiece (1627), without argument; the plates' Latin apparatus instead traces to Tycho's own publications (De mundi aetherei 1588; Epistolae astronomicae 1596), and a museum-affiliated inventory dates the object 1623, before the claimed model.
Original or copy
A posthumous derivative of contested lineage. Its Latin apparatus traces to Tycho's own publications (the 1588 De mundi heading recast in the third person; the Stjerneborg motto printed 1596); the 2006 caption's derivation from the Rudolphine frontispiece — where 'Quid si sic' also appears, as Kepler's plate quoting Tycho — is unargued, unverified for the figures (both stand here; the frontispiece's Copernicus sits), and impossible if the competing 1623 date is right. The paired-portrait scheme has a precedent in Tycho's own instrument decoration (Mechanica 1598). It remains the Iconographia's example of Tycho figured as proprietor of a cosmology rather than as a face.
Dated 1596 → 1642
The plates' Latin traces to Tycho's own prints (the 1588 De mundi heading recast third-person; the motto printed 1596); 1627 is a lower bound only if the caption's unverified pictorial derivation holds, and is impossible if the competing unfooted 1623 date (Frombork inventory) is right. 1642 rests on the unfootnoted 2006 caption; the museum record is undated and no engraved date or signature is visible in the 2006 photograph.
Whereabouts

On display at the National Museum in Copenhagen, in the Renaissance galleries.

HolderNationalmuseet, Danmarks Middelalder og Renæssance, Copenhagen
InventoryD138
Evidencelocated located (record-level page on the museum's own database)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

The museum's record carries no object date — its 'Datering' field shows 1970–2014, which is photographic file metadata. The 1642 date rests on the 2006 caption alone. The museum's own image filename calls the plates 'Messing Døre', brass doors. Hinge-knuckle hardware and facing lock escutcheons visible in the 2006 photograph suggest the leaves are lockable doors, supporting the 'Messing Døre' filename against the catalogued 'sideplader'. The museum's image asset is persistently server-broken (HTTP 500, no Wayback copy), so the 2006 published photograph is the working surrogate. The Frombork instrument inventory dates the object 1623 without stated source.

Heinrich Hansen, 1882 — Tycho as national history painting

afterlifeextantAicon-hansen-1882
1882
'Uranienborg på Hveen': Anders Sørensen Vedel presents the first Danish collection of ballads to Queen Sophie in Tycho's presence. The museum's A 154, a nocturne of Stjerneborg, is by the same artist, from the same year, and hangs in the same room — Hven by day and by night — and both canvases sit within the museum's founding-era history-painting programme (J. C. Jacobsen's foundation, royal decree of 5 April 1878; the programme abandoned c. 1890, per Eller 1978); but no commission record has been recovered for either canvas, and no source uses pair or pendant language. By the 1880s Tycho had become a figure of national costume drama, and this is the best-evidenced example: the museum, not merely the book, names him as a sitter.
Maker
Heinrich Hansen (1821–1890), architectural painter.
Original or copy
A 19th-century history painting — an invention, not a likeness. Which figure is Tycho rests on the museum's metadata and the depicted narrative (corroborated by Eller 1978, p. 43), not on physiognomy: at this scale no head carries a discriminator of either the engraved or the painted tradition, and no source documents Hansen's model.
Dated 1882 → 1882
Dated by the museum's catalogue record; corroborated by Eller 1978 (p. 43, 'Rekonstruktion af Heinrich Hansen 1882') and the 'H.H. 82' monogram reported on the sister version.
Whereabouts

Hanging at Frederiksborg, Room 27.

HolderDet Nationalhistoriske Museum på Frederiksborg, Hillerød, Denmark
InventoryA 153
Evidencelocated located (record-level page; the museum's 'afbildede personer' field names Brahe, Queen Sophie and Vedel)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

A 154, the Stjerneborg nocturne, names no sitters in the museum's record — confirmed on the museum's own page for it (frederiksborg.dk/kunstvaerk/a-154/), a thinner record with no cross-reference to A 153 — and no figure in it can be identified, so it is recorded here rather than as a separate depiction. The museum publishes only a 210 × 202 px image of A 153; the high-resolution surrogate on Wikimedia Commons is the sister version Hansen also painted (Bruun Rasmussen 2005; evidently Phillips, Loeb collection, 2026), not A 153.

Eckersberg's sketch, 1831 — the young Christian IV visiting Hven

afterlifeuntracedCicon-eckersberg-1831
1831 or later, by 1842
A pencil sketch by Eckersberg, captioned in 2006 as a study for his history painting of the young Christian IV visiting Tycho Brahe on Hven. The bearded man at the globe is the Tycho figure. Eckersberg treated the subject across a documented 1831–1842 series — a first sketch laid out 31 January 1831 (initially the gold-chain moment), the finished painting, an oil sketch, two etchings, and an 1842 woodcut design for the atlas to Ursin's survey of the graphic arts — and his treatment appears isolated in 19th-century Danish painting; whether this sheet is preparatory or derivative within the series is undetermined.
Maker
C. W. Eckersberg.
Original or copy
A 19th-century invention. No claim to likeness.
Dated 1831 → 1842
The caption dates the related history painting to 1831 (diary: painting finished 23 March 1831; etchings May–June 1831; SMK KKS2591 inscribed May 1831). The sheet itself is undated: preparatory if the caption's 'til historiemaleri' framing is right (before March 1831), later if it belongs to the print campaigns, whose last documented drawing episode is 18 June 1842 (the block drawing for Flinch).
Whereabouts

Attested at Frederiksborg on a single 2006 credit line (Fig. 2, p. 247). Characterised triple negative: the 2006 volume has no picture-credit apparatus (its back matter is a Litteraturliste only); absent from Frederiksborg's fully-enumerated published catalogue (c. 1,791 sitemap records) — which the museum itself states excludes its drawings holdings; absent from SMK (three etchings, zero drawings), from Weilbach, and from the demonstrably incomplete national aggregator (samlinger.slks.dk).

HolderDet Nationalhistoriske Museum på Frederiksborg (attested only), Hillerød, Denmark
Evidenceattested attested — one 2006 credit line, no inventory number, no reproduction located online
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

The reproduction is Fig. 2 on printed p. 247 — printed p. 245 carries Fig. 1, Heinrich Hansen's 1882 Vedel/Sophie painting, so prior citations of p. 245 for this sheet are wrong. The museum's public catalogue is a selection and does not publish its drawings (institutionally confirmed: the museum states its drawings and prints cannot be permanently exhibited).

Open questions 5
  1. The sheet's inventory number, accession history, medium and dimensions, which only Frederiksborg can supply — the one remaining route from attested to located.
  2. Is the sheet a preparatory drawing, or a transfer/derivative drawing of the print campaigns — including its relation to the 18 June 1842 block drawing for Flinch?
  3. Villadsen's 2009 edition of Eckersberg's diaries (print only) and Fischer 1983, Tegninger af C.W. Eckersberg, remain unconsulted.
  4. The 27 February 2018 auction of the 26 × 17 cm oil (probably Hannover no. 441) is unidentified; the auction house's record is not public.
  5. Post-1992 whereabouts of the painting (Hannover no. 438) after Helge Pedersen.

30

The Levy memorial sheet — Tycho for children

afterlifeuntracedCicon-levy-mindeblad
undated; 'måske 1901?'
A 'Historisk Mindeblad' — a historical memorial sheet for children — from the series 'Danske Billeder for smaae og store Börn', no. 3, both series line and number printed on the sheet itself. The sheet is a full 3×3 picture grid: a central column of an oval bust lettered TYCHO. BRAHE. * 14 December 1546 + 24 October 1601 (the lettering confirmed exact), a large Uranienborg elevation in a moated formal garden, and a six-line verse panel over a sleeping dog, flanked by eight uncaptioned genre vignettes — instruments, a pair of armillary spheres, women at a spinning wheel, garden and reading scenes, figures in Renaissance pastiche dress. The bust is bare-headed and corresponds to the standing type (aetatis 50), not the plumed-cap de Gheyn engraved type — a visual comparison only, with no intermediate print traced. The verse reads: 'I længst forsvundne Dage Uranienborg hist laae, / Paa Hveen, hvor Tycho Brahe op paa de Stjerner saae, / Saa riig, saa skjøn og pragtfuld den stod, som her Du seer, / Men ak! den sank i Gruset, nu Borgen er ei meer. / Vi gjemme kun dens Billed og Mindet om den Mand, / Som var og altid bliver en Stolthed for vort Land!' This is Tycho as schoolroom furniture: the point at which the engineered image had become a national children's story.
Maker
Published by A. Levy, Copenhagen — the sheet itself is imprinted 'Forlagt af A. Levy.' (two independent 600-dpi readings of the 2006 reproduction; 'Levison' is incompatible with the glyphs), but the firm is untraced in every reached Danish trade source: the name rests on the image alone.
Original or copy
A 19th-century popular derivative of the portrait tradition (the central bust follows the bare-headed standing type rather than the de Gheyn engravings). One physical sheet.
Dated c. 1855 → 1901
The sheet carries no date. The 2006 caption guesses 'måske 1901?' — with the question mark, which must be preserved; the guess is undefended in print, though it sits adjacent to the volume's ten pages of Prague-1901 material. Orthography and diction narrow nothing: 'ei' and 'smaae' are long-lived literary archaisms (ODS s.vv. 'ej', 'smaa'), and 'Uranienborg' is Tycho's own original name-form, used here as an antiquarian gesture. No publisher dates for any 'A. Levy' have been found in any consulted source, so the c. 1855 lower bound is conventional, not evidenced. The bounds remain wide for want of evidence.
Whereabouts

Attested in Nationalmuseet's topographical archive on the museum's own 2006 statement; no live record found.

HolderNationalmuseet, Antikvarisk-Topografisk Arkiv (ATA), Copenhagen
Evidenceattested attested — the 2006 caption only; the museum's collections database returns nothing
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

Mortensen 1946 does not cover the sheet — verified by exhaustive full-text search of the whole Cassiopeia 1946 volume (no hit for the sheet, the series, or any Levy), not merely inferred from his scope — and it cannot be the source of the caption's '1901?'. Mortensen also published a 1949 iconography survey, not yet checked.

Open questions 5
  1. 'A. Levy' is unidentified in the Danish trade record — Kraks Vejviser c. 1855–1905, Dansk Bogfortegnelse, Dansk Boghandlertidende, the Royal Library's Småtrykssamling; other Levy first names should be tried, since 'A.' alone is thin.
  2. A full-text search of the verse lines in Mediestream would give a terminus post quem for the poem independent of the publisher question; the Copenhagen papers of October–December 1901 may carry advertisements for Tycho commemorative sheets, and the Nationaltidende article of 23 September 1901 has not been read.
  3. Whether the Antikvarisk-Topografisk Arkiv holds the physical sheet or a photograph only, under what journal or inventory number, and whether a high-resolution scan can be had.
  4. The intermediate nineteenth-century print behind the bare-headed oval bust is untraced; it would give an independent terminus post quem.
  5. Mortensen's 1949 iconography survey is unchecked, as is whether any register of Alfred Jacobsen's 'Danske Billeder' documents a no. 3.
Relations
  • depicts Tycho Brahe
  • reconstructed derivation of The aetatis-50 portrait (Skokloster / Edinburgh) — the exile likeness §6
