31

The full-figure watercolour at Strahov — the standing Tycho with a sextant

afterlifeuntracedCicon-progymnasmata-watercolour
c. 1598 – 1666; the drawing's date is open
A watercolour on parchment, in folio, bound in front of a copy of the Astronomiae instauratae progymnasmata at Strahov — a second book Tycho presented to the same Bohemian magnate who received the Strahov Mechanica. The composition is unlike every other painted Tycho: a full-length standing figure on a plinth, leaning on a sextant, in a beret and a long cloak reaching to the base, the whole reading as the depiction of a statue rather than of a man. Professor Šafařík distributed photolithographs of it to those present at the astronomical society's Strasbourg meeting in 1881, describing it as the only original portrait of Tycho in Prague.
Maker
Unknown. Friis raises the possibility that it is by Philip Kilian, which would make it seventeenth-century and no witness to Tycho at all — 'Hvem Tegningen skyldes, vides ikke; maaske er den af Kilian.'
Original or copy
Genuinely open, and the interesting question about it. Friis observes that the photolithograph agrees with Philip Kilian's engraving in Historia Coelestis (ed. Albert Curtz as 'Lucius Barrettus', Augsburg 1666) in both form and size so closely that the drawing may be the very model for the plate — but equally it may be after it, or by Kilian himself. Friis's own view is that the ultimate original behind both is de Gheyn's engraving. Šafařík's 1881 description of it as 'the only original portrait of Tycho in Prague' should not be relied on. A first comparison against Kilian's plate (made here, at matched figure height) confirms beyond doubt that the two are the same composition, in the same direction, with the same pose, instrument, beret, ruff, fur-lined cloak and stance. It does not settle direction: the engraving is more elaborate throughout — a larger plume, a more structured beret, a more articulated instrument — which is what one expects both of an engraver elaborating a simpler model AND of a line engraving beside a halftone of a watercolour. The question stays open. A further comparison adds one datum: the two are same-sense. Six of six calibrated Netherlandish painting-to-engraving pairs reverse, so a same-sense pair leans against the watercolour having served as a face-up-traced production model for the plate. Two caveats keep this a lean rather than a verdict: the observation is made on the 1901 wrapper, whose undocumented reproduction chain may itself flip orientation; and the rule is calibrated on a different workshop tradition seventy to eighty years earlier — Kilian's own transfer practice is undocumented, and blackened-verso and counterproof transfers print same-sense. The direction question thus leans against Friis's model reading without being settled.
Dated c. 1598 → 1666
The lower bound is the book it is bound into — a copy of the Progymnasmata that Tycho gave away, with his autograph dedication to Johann Zajíc von Hasenburk. But Friis warns that a drawing bound into a presented book need not be contemporary with the book: 'naar Vandfarvetegningen nu findes i en Bog, som Tyge Brahe har bortforæret, saa følger dog ikke heraf, at den er samtidig med Bogen.' The upper bound is Philip Kilian's engraving in Historia coelestis (Augsburg: Utzschneider, 1666, ed. Albert Curtz as 'Lucius Barrettus'), which the drawing either precedes as its model or follows as a derivative — Friis's 'Vienna 1657' imprint for the plate is refuted, and no documented 1657 issue exists. The 1881 report is now read at the source rather than through Friis: Vierteljahrsschrift der Astronomischen Gesellschaft 16, p. 273, in the Bayerische Staatsbibliothek's digitisation.
Whereabouts

At Strahov, in a presentation copy of the Progymnasmata — a different book from the Strahov Mechanica.

HolderStrahovská knihovna, Královská kanonie premonstrátů na Strahově, Prague, Czech Republic
Evidenceattested attested — Friis and the Vierteljahrsschrift der Astronomischen Gesellschaft (1881); no live Czech record reached
Recordno online record for this object has been reached

Distinct from the Strahov Mechanica. Both were presented by Tycho to Jan Zbyněk Zajíc z Házmburka, so the same recipient holds two Tycho presentation volumes, each carrying a watercolour portrait — and the two portraits are of different types. Status is 'untraced', not 'extant': the object's survival rests on the 1881 report and nothing later. A third independent silence has since been added: Boldan & Vackářová, Bibliotheca astronomica (Národní knihovna ČR, 2024), pp. 88–91 — a Strahov-focused Tycho chapter — illustrates only the Mechanica and returns no trace of this Progymnasmata volume, the strongest of the three silences, 143 years after the report. The same source gives the sibling Mechanica's current shelfmark, AG XI 56. Neither the Strahov catalogue (Katalog Tritius) nor Manuscriptorium could be queried for this edition — the live-record check is unexecuted, not negative. Friis's imprint for the Kilian engraving, 'Vienna 1657', is refuted — see the Kilian record.

Where the sources disagree 2
QuestionPositionsStatus
Is the drawing the model for Kilian's engraving, or a copy of it?Model — Friis notes the photolithograph and the engraving agree in form and size so exactly that 'det maaske netop er denne Tegning, hvorefter Stikket er udført'. Derivative — the drawing may equally be by Kilian, in which case it postdates the plate in conception.unresolved, and it decides whether this is a lifetime object or a seventeenth-century one — the engraving is now recorded separately as icon-kilian-standing, with its plate at Wellcome 1357i, so the comparison Friis made by eye can be made properly as soon as an image of the watercolour is obtained. Dreyer endorses the model reading twice — Tycho Brahe (1890), printed p. 264, the sole occurrence in the book (the circulating citation 'notes p. 288' is an error), and again, unhedged, in his preface to Opera Omnia X (1923, via Meddelelser fra Ole Rømers Venner 14/1, 2006, p. 31) — but both endorsements root in the single 1881 Šafařík report: endorsement, not independent evidence. The same-sense finding recorded under nature leans against the model reading, carried with its two caveats.
Does the Studnička wrapper reproduce the watercolour, or Kilian's engraving?For the watercolour: the wrapper has the plain slab and the facsimile signature, which are Friis's stated differentiators, and a facsimile signature is a positive feature that cannot be produced by cropping an engraving. Against certainty: the wrapper is a 1901 halftone, and Studnička nowhere says what he reproduced.well-supported but with undocumented chain of custody — Friis's differentiators (plain slab, facsimile 'Tycho Brahe') are his own observations from a copy of the 1881 photolithograph in hand, absent from the Vierteljahrsschrift minutes he quotes, and neither feature can be produced by cropping or retouching the engraving; whether the wrapper prints from the 1881 plate or a fresh reproduction of the same original remains undocumented
Open questions 4
  1. Is Studnička's wrapper a print from Šafařík's 1881 plate, or a fresh 1901 reproduction of the same original? Studnička's text is silent.
  2. Studnička's catalogue documents the Strahov Mechanica copy in detail but never mentions this Progymnasmata volume or the 1881 episode. The silence is now partially explained: his item C.I footnote borrows an illustration from a Progymnasmata, 1603, at Strahov, so a copy was accessible to him in 1900–01 — which weakens any inference that the volume had left Strahov by 1901 — and his catalogue's scope (ownership-relics of Tycho's own library) plausibly excludes a presentation gift, though that genre explanation is a candidate reading, not established. Whether the accessible copy is the same volume is unknown.
  3. No sighting of the Progymnasmata volume after 1881 has been located; Boldan & Vackářová's Strahov-focused Tycho chapter (2024) adds a third independent silence. Whether it is still at Strahov is unverified — the live-record check against Katalog Tritius and Manuscriptorium remains unexecuted, not negative.
  4. A comparison of the wrapper against Kilian's plate at higher resolution than a 1901 halftone allows: the direction of dependence is still open, and the halftone is too coarse to settle it.
Relations
  • depicts Tycho Brahe
  • compare with Kilian's standing Tycho (1666) — a whole body invented around a borrowed head
  • disputed model for Kilian's standing Tycho (1666) — a whole body invented around a borrowed head §6

32

Kilian's standing Tycho (1666) — a whole body invented around a borrowed head

afterlifeextantAicon-kilian-standing
engraved after July 1658; published 1666
Tycho at full length, standing on an architectural pedestal and leaning on a large triangular sighting instrument, in a plumed beret and a fur-lined cloak falling to the plinth. Dreyer and Friis both call the instrument a sextant, but no graduated arc is visible in either examined impression; the open frame more nearly resembles Tycho's parallactic-ruler (triangulum) type, and no positive match to any Mechanica plate has been made — the identification is uncertain. (The small mark at the frame's base is joinery — a peg and foot-bracket, with a matching pivot-pin at the apex — not a letter or plate-mark.) The whole composition reads as the depiction of a statue rather than of a living man, and that is roughly what it is: a monument invented sixty-five years after his death, for the first printed edition of his observation logs.
Maker
Philip Kilian (Augsburg, 1628–1693, son of Wolfgang Kilian), signed at the foot of the pedestal 'Philip Kilian sculps.'
Original or copy
A posthumous invention, and demonstrably a composite. The pedestal inscription is not Kilian's: it is transcribed from de Gheyn's engraved portrait — a bust, in a different composition — with a single lexical substitution, instrumentorumque Astronomicorum … dispositorum for de Gheyn's Machinarumque Astronomicarum … dispositarum. Everything else runs word for word, including 'Aetatis suæ anno 40. Anno Dñi. 1586 compl.' The consequence matters: the 1586 and the age of 40 on this plate are inherited text, not evidence about the standing figure. Anyone dating this portrait type from its own inscription is dating a borrowing. Direct examination of two impressions shows every checkable costume element reading the de Gheyn v2 scheme — medal absent, beret worn and plumed, a single doubled chain, the gripping hand at the viewer's left — and none reading v1; but the v2 scheme is also attested in the drawing family and was the dominant circulating type, so this fixes the costume vocabulary, not which carrier Kilian copied. Prandtl (1932) moreover reads the nose-prosthesis on this plate — a feature present in neither de Gheyn state — which, if his reading holds, would mean the head is not a pure de Gheyn transfer; the reading is unconfirmed at available resolution and is carried as Prandtl's, not as observed fact.
Dated 18 July 1658 → 1666
The lower bound comes from the plate's companion. The same gathering carries a double-page frontispiece by the same hand, signed identically, showing four Habsburg emperors with putti holding banderoles — PARAVIT, …PAVIT, RECEPIT, PVBLICAVIT — a programme that culminates in the reigning emperor under whom the book was published, Leopold I. Leopold was not elected until 18 July 1658, so no plate in that gathering can predate it. The upper bound is publication: Historia Coelestis, Augsburg, Simon Utzschneider, 1666.
Whereabouts

An edition multiple, in copies of the 1666 Historia Coelestis, and separately catalogued as a print.

HolderWellcome Collection, London
Inventory1357i (Miro V0000748)
Evidencelocated located (record-level catalogue page, IIIF at 2123 × 3272, Public Domain Mark)
Recordthe holding institution’s own record ↗

Burgess, Portraits of Doctors & Scientists in the Wellcome Institute (1973), no. 405.9. Further impressions: the Royal Danish Library copy of the book, Fol. Astr. 35430, digitised at leaf 18; and a copy in the trade photographed at high resolution, whose plate agrees with the Wellcome impression in every detail including the signature — same plate, same state. The 1672 Ratisbonae cancel-title reissue carries the portrait plate: the K10plus/VD17 collation gives '[1] Portr. (Kupferst.)' with a note identifying the edition as a reprint of Augsburg 1666 (the printer is given as Emmrich in that single record, uncorroborated). A further impression: Royal Society, MS/648, Turnor Collectanea Newtoniana, plate 273 × 174 mm (pictures.royalsociety.org/image-rs-17166). Caution: Wellcome's own catalogue dates the print 1586, straight from the borrowed plinth inscription — an institutional instance of this record's inherited-text warning.

InscriptionEffigies Tychonis Brahe Ottonidis Dani, Domini de Knudstrup, et arcis Vranienburg in Insula Hellesponti Danici Huænna Fundatoris, instrumentorúmque Astronomicorum in eâdem dispositorum inventoris et structoris. Aetatis suæ anno 40. Anno Dñi. 1586 compl.
Where the sources disagree 2
QuestionPositionsStatus
Vienna 1657 or Augsburg 1666?Friis cites the plate as belonging to 'Historia coelestis … Wien 1657, i Folio'. Two distinct printings exist and he has conflated them: a small pilot volume, Sylloge Ferdinandea, with a 1656 title-page and a colophon naming David Hautt at Vienna in 1657, containing only the preface, Liber Prolegomenon and the 1582 observations; and the full Augsburg 1666 folio, which alone carries the three large plates. Mortensen 1946 perpetuates Friis's Vienna-1657 label in his p. 138 prose ('i Historia coelestis, Wien 1657') while his own table at p. 80 correctly gives 'P. Kilians Stik 1666' — the error has a documented transmission chain, Friis → Mortensen.settled against Friis, twice over: the pilot volume has no such plates, and the Leopold I argument makes 1657 chronologically impossible. Cite Augsburg 1666.
Is the Strahov watercolour Kilian's model, or a copy after his plate?Strahov held two distinct Tycho watercolours, routinely conflated: one in the Mechanica presentation copy (recorded by Biela in 1824; today AG XI 56) and one in a Progymnasmata copy, published in photolithograph by Šafařík in 1881 — only the second is in dispute here. Friis noted that the photolithograph agrees with this engraving in form and size so exactly that the drawing may be the very model — but added that it may equally be by Kilian himself. One asymmetry is suggestive rather than decisive: the watercolour reportedly shows a plain rectangular slab with 'Tycho Brahe' in facsimile beneath the figure, where the engraving has a full architectural pedestal carrying the borrowed inscription; adding a pedestal and importing an inscription is ordinary engraver's elaboration, stripping one back is not what a copyist usually does. Against the model reading now stands Friis's own Addenda (p. 420): Kraus and Studnička informed him privately that the photolithograph reproduced a drawn copy after Kilian's plate, which 'ikke mere findes der' — no longer found at Strahov. That testimony collides directly with Studnička 1901 (p. 60), which states in print that the Progymnasmata volume was preserved in the Strahov library; neither statement can be shown to supersede the other. Friis's own considered verdict, never retracted, is that drawing and engraving are independent siblings, both descending from de Gheyn — a judgment resting on the recurring inscription, that is on textual, not compositional, evidence. A final caution gates all prior comparisons: the reproductions in Studnička 1901 (pp. 44–48) are anchored, by Friis's Addenda and independently by Norlind's Mechanica census, to the Mechanica watercolour, not the disputed Progymnasmata one — so every side-by-side comparison made from that surrogate is provisional.still open, but rebalanced: documented testimony — Kraus and Studnička via Friis's addendum, and Studnička 1901's silence on the watercolour among his catalogued genuine Tychoniana — now leans against Strahov-as-model, without reaching dispositive: the reversal is method-less hearsay about an object already missing when it was reported, and collides with Studnička's own printed 'preserved'. The question still decides whether the Strahov watercolour is a lifetime object or a seventeenth-century one.
Open questions 6
  1. The plate's number in Westergaard, Danske portrætter i kobberstik (1930–33) — still not established. Only entries 1375–1382 have been consulted, short of any Kilian entry, and no number is inferred.
  2. What the reproductions in Studnička 1901 (pp. 44–48) and the 1901 wrapper actually depict, at caption level — the question that gates every surrogate comparison of the plate against the Strahov watercolour.
  3. The identity of 'Kraus' (Prague, before 1901), and Šafařík's 1881 account at its primary source.
  4. A live Strahov record for the Progymnasmata/Zajíc copy has not been reached; the post-1901 fate of both volume and watercolour is untested.
  5. The instrument's typological identity — no graduated arc is visible, and no positive match to a Mechanica plate has been made.
  6. Hollstein's Kilian volumes, for a systematic survey of his signature practice; Reznicek I p. 303 (the Schwarz portrait) unread.
Relations
  • depicts Tycho Brahe
  • documented derivation of The posthumous copies (1602 onward) §6
  • disputed derivation of The full-figure watercolour at Strahov — the standing Tycho with a sextant §6

4The objects, by holding institution

Where the thing itself is. This section re-projects the register along the axis of custody: every physical object or exemplar the edition has located or attested — the primary object of each record and every version and exemplar its record enumerates — grouped by holding institution, with inventory number, support and size where known, the verdict on it (original, copy, cast, reproduction, lost), and the institution’s own record where one is online. Objects that no longer exist are listed under the place where they perished. The question the table answers and the catalogue cannot is what does this institution hold: Frederiksborg, for instance, holds a candidate for the Steensgaard panel, a small copy of the burned oil, a cast of the Prague effigy, Heinrich Hansen’s history painting and, on one attestation, an Eckersberg sheet.

Characterised triple negative: the 2006 volume has no picture-credit apparatus (its back matter is a Litteraturliste only); absent from Frederiksborg's fully-enumerated published catalogue (c. 1,791 sitemap records) — which the museum itself states excludes its drawings holdings; absent from SMK (three etchings, zero drawings), from Weilbach, and from the demonstrably incomplete national aggregator (samlinger.slks.dk).none reached

Recorded by Peder Hansen Resen, Inscriptiones Hafnienses (1668), pp. 335–336, whence its inscription is now read in full: first person ('restitutus fui'), without the 'divino provisu' clause, placed and dated VANDESBECHI AN. MDXCVII, and signed TYCHO BRAHE OT. — where the surviving canvases' caption is third-person, unplaced and undated. Mortensen's list gives its two recorded descendants as the Bodleian and Gavnø copies, but that is his list annotation only: his own prose (p. 71) asserts 'samme Inskription' across the Copenhagen and Edinburgh pictures — one chain, not branches, and wrong in detail, since the texts differ — and the shared pyramid/cone emblem connects the Copenhagen picture to the surviving canvases; the separate-branch stemma should be dropped.

In print: Danske Magazin II (Copenhagen 1746)

1 object
RecordWhatInventorySupport and sizeVerdictLink
The Týn church epitaph, Prague — the effigy that closes the seriesversion / exemplarcopper engraving, printed on the text pagethe earliest printed record of the monument, and the witness to its pre-1901 state
Attributed to Johann Martin Bernigeroth by the 2006 volume; the plate as printed carries no engraver's signature legible at 600 dpi and the 1746 text names none, so the attribution is not hardened here. The 1746 text documents the production chain: drawn in 1743 in Prague for Tycho de Hofman, the Danish Chancery secretary there for Maria Theresa's Bohemian coronation, and engraved for his Portraits historiques (The Hague, 1746), the plate then lent to Danske Magazin (bind 2, pp. 356–57) — naming the commissioner but no engraver. The Bernigeroth attribution traces to Vellev's fig.-3 caption alone, and his 'efter Danske Magazin' could even describe a later derivative. A collation trap: it is printed on the text page at the head of p. 353, not on an inserted leaf.
Grinder-Hansen ed. 2006, fig. 3; autopsy of the 1746 volume

5Dating: the bounds and their arguments

One row per record that carries dated bounds: the earliest and the latest it can be, and the argument that fixes each bound. The bounds are only as strong as their arguments, which is why the argument is printed beside each pair and not summarised. The chart draws the same bounds on one scale, by series, so that the shape of the campaign is visible: the likeness layer of 1586–87, the die of 1588, the two Amsterdam plates of 1590 and 1595, the exile type of 1597 and the last watercolours of 1598–1600, then the long tail of the afterlife. Records with no bounds — the arms stamp, the inserted book portrait, the printed views, the posthumous copies — are dated by their carriers and appear in the catalogue with their year range only.

RecordNo earlier thanNo later thanThe argumentGrade
The portraits on the equatorial armillary's pedestalc. 1584December 1596 (the Icones instrumentorum issue)Name-labels N·C·T·G / T·BR·D sit on the entablatures above the niches; the year-tablets on the plinths beneath the figures read '…520' (leading digit obscured by the block's own carved bracket in every impression examined) and '1580' ('I580' is the font's flag-serif numeral 1). The years are floruit labels, settled by direct inspection of Nathan Chytraeus, Variorum in Europa itinerum deliciae (Herborn 1594), pp. 596–598 (inspected August 2026): the Stjerneborg hypocaustum cycle — Tycho's own sibling lineage programme, on material his preface says came largely from Tycho himself — gives every figure an 'Anno X' floruit line, with COPERNICUS 'Anno 1520 post Christum' and TYCHO BRAHE 'Anno 1580': the identical pair of years the pedestal tablets carry. The pedestal therefore uses the same convention (and the obscured '…520' is confirmed as 1520); '1580' cannot in any case date the execution, since no armillary existed then. Christianson 2000's reading of 1580 as a construction year (against his own 2020 chronology) is thereby retired; what 1580 commemorates within Tycho's career (Uraniborg's completion is Dreyer's candidate) remains open, and Zeeberg 1995 ('The Inscriptions at Tycho Brahe's Uraniborg', in Skafte Jensen ed., Odense 1995, pp. 251–66 — undigitised; no scan located as of August 2026) is now corroborative rather than load-bearing. If Christianson's hedged Gemperlin attribution were adopted, the upper bound would fall to 1587; it stays 1598 (publication of the recording plate). The 1585 Icones instrumentorum (Nielsen 976, Uraniborg broadsides, Marburg unicum — enclosed with Tycho's 1 March 1586 letter to Wilhelm IV) was checked as a possible earlier attestation (August 2026): its armillary woodcut depicts the steel armilla (Fig. 13 type; 'pes...ex solido metallo'), not this instrument — a diagnostic null that leaves the 1598 plate the earliest witness to the portraits. The hard upper bound is December 1596, not 1598: the Icones instrumentorum suite (Nielsen 975, Uraniborg 1596, 18 letterpress-free woodcut pulls issued with some Epistolae copies; presentation copies to Christian IV and Chancellor Friis in December 1596) contains the armillary printed from what is almost certainly the same woodblock (Stockholm KB exemplar examined leaf-by-leaf: identical tablets, V-medallion, and the full compositor letter-key in identical positions; print-flaw-level confirmation pending an uncoloured 1598 impression). The painting window is therefore summer 1584 to December 1596, preferred sub-window 1584–87 unchanged. The block itself was cut somewhere between 1584 and 1596; the documented production waves (two blocks 1585, two 1586–87 for De mundi, several 1592–93 for the Progymnasmata, boxwood-only from April 1590) make the post-1590 campaign the likeliest home for it, stated as preference.A
The Kronborg tapestry figure — the conjectured earliest likeness15811584The dating is the one certainty: the royal genealogy tapestry series for Kronborg was woven 1581–1584, so the figure — whoever he is — falls in that window on hard documentary grounds. Everything else is conditional: the identification as Tycho, first proposed by Wanscher (Kronborgs Historie, 1939; Mortensen's 'Kbh. 1930' is a probable citation error — Wanscher's 1930 book is Rosenborgs Historie), rests on facial resemblance in a small background figure and has never been proven ('possibly correct' was Mortensen's verdict). If the identification holds, this is the earliest surviving likeness of Tycho, made when he was 35–38.C
The Steensgaard panel — a lifetime portrait or an early copy, never testedc. 1584c. 1588These bounds date the likeness the panel carries, not the panel itself, whose status is genuinely open. The costume places the likeness in the mid-1580s: Tycho wears the Frederik II medal and not the Order of the Elephant. That argument is weaker than it looks and should not be leaned on hard — the year Tycho received the Elephant is nowhere documented (Friis: he must have had it from Frederik II, 'but when, or on what occasion, is not known'), and the usual 'c.1585/86' is itself inferred from portraits, so dating portraits by it is circular. What the panel plainly is not is a picture of the later Tycho: it shows neither the Elephant of the 1586–87 oil nor the stacked chains of the engravings.C
The Stjerneborg astronomer cycle — Tycho as the terminus of his own science15841594Stjerneborg was built in 1584, so nothing could be painted in its warming room before that. The completed eight-figure cycle, including the Tychonides panel, is described as painted — icons with gold-lettered distichs in the hypocaustum — in Nathan Chytraeus, Variorum in Europa itinerum deliciae (Herborn 1594), pp. 596–598, on material Chytraeus's preface says came largely from Tycho himself; Tycho's own fuller printed description follows in the Epistolae astronomicae (1596). The painting campaign in the meteorological diary falls in spring 1591, inside the window but tied to this cycle by no source.A
The Frederiksborg oil (attributed to Gemperlin) — known through C. A. Jensen's 1838 copylate 15861587The lower bound rests on Gassendi's report (1655 ed., p. 137) that toward the end of 1586 Tycho 'wished to be depicted' by Gemperlin — voluit, a volitional construction, not a flat report of a completed sitting; the 'fortieth birthday' precision sometimes attached to this is not in Gassendi's narrative but belongs to the caption of the volume's unrelated frontispiece print. The burned picture's own inscription, as reported by Friis (Bidrag til dansk Kunsthistorie, p. 229), 'Anno Domini 1587 aetatis suae 40', independently corroborates the 1586/87 window; a sweep of the TBOO correspondence for 1586–88 is negative. The Order of the Elephant the sitter wears supplies only a floor: Christianson (2020, p. 97) dates Tycho's grant to 3 May 1580 at Odense (quote verified; his footnote's archival source not yet traced), so the device gives a hard 1580 terminus post quem rather than a mid-1580s one. The upper bound is firmer: the painter's death. Tycho's own De mundi aetherei II (printed at Uraniborg 1587) records Gemperlin dead of the plague — so if the attribution holds, the panel cannot postdate 1587.B
The woodcut 'Effigies' (Westergaard 1376) — the praedelineatum's recordlate 1586February 1592The lower bound is the inscribed design date — 'aetatis 40 … 1586' names the sitting/design year, tied to Tycho's 40th birthday in December 1586. The upper bound is documentary: the block was in use on the title verso of the Progymnasmata prospectus distributed with letters of 20 February – 22 March 1592. The physical cutting date is open anywhere between those bounds. The 1588–90 wood-cutter recruitment letters concern instrument figures, never the portrait, and cannot anchor the block; the 25 August 1585 Hagecius letter shows cutting capacity intermittent and partly off-island. Both de Gheyn engravings retain the 1586 tablet years later — in this family the inscribed date travels with the design, not the object.B
The Prague panel — Mortensen's Clementinum portrait1586openThe panel, painted on wood and held in the astronomical museum in the Clementinum at Prague, is unsigned and carries the same composition and the same inscription as the 1586 woodcut, so the two are certainly connected. Which came first is the question. Mortensen argued the panel is the woodcut's model rather than a copy of it, resting chiefly on a detail of the hands: the painting shows two rings that the woodcut leaves out, and a copyist adds detail rather than removing it. If he is right the panel dates from about 1586 and is the closest surviving thing to the painted stage behind the whole print campaign; if he is wrong the panel could be considerably later. He was himself tentative, and the record follows him without asserting him. Two further limits now stand on that question. The ring count — the sole load-bearing evidence for the model thesis — is untestable at the surviving reproduction's confirmed native maximum of 2200 × 3190 px: two independent attempts left no countable ring bands on either hand, so Mortensen's evidence is currently unverifiable in either direction. And since the panel's existence is itself attested by Mortensen alone, the existence question is logically prior to the dating question.C
The mural quadrant portrait (Uraniborg)15821587The great mural quadrant itself was built in 1582 — nothing could be painted in its arc before that. The date 1587 is not merely conventional: it is inscribed on the plate's own tablet ('A° DOMINI 1587 ÆTATIS SVÆ 40', confirmed letter-for-letter at 600 DPI in two independent KB Copenhagen copies), and Tycho's text endorses the tablet ('prout vides'). The cap on Gemperlin's share of the work comes from his death — dead by 1587, the die-window running 1586 to May 1588 — not from Tycho's testimony. For the effigy and the architecture the attribution rests on Tycho's own word: he named Gemperlin and Stenwinkel outright, and praised the likeness in the Mechanica ('vix similior dari possit' — scarcely could a more faithful one be made); the landscape hand he named only as 'Johannes of Antwerp, royal painter at Kronborg', the surname Knieper being inherited, uncontested scholarship. A genuine late-1586 strand — Gassendi (via Resen 1668, attached to this mural effigy specifically), Thoren 1990, and Friis's earlier essay ('omtrent 1586') — dates the execution to late 1586; under aetatis-40 reckoning (Tycho's 40th year ran 14 December 1586 – 13 December 1587) both dates are consistent with a sitting late in 1586 completed by 1587.A
The gilt portrait supralibros — two dieslate 158620 March 1588The upper bound is an object: the Royal Library's Sascerides copy of De mundi aetherei (LN 428 4°, eks. 1) carries both gilt ovals — portrait on the front cover, arms on the back — on its original parchment presentation binding, with Tycho's autograph dedication to Gellius Sascerides dated 20 March 1588 (verified on the EEB surrogate), so both dies existed by that date. The British Library's Maestlin copy, dispatched from Uraniborg on 14 May 1588, remains the second keystone — nobody rebinds a book into a Tycho presentation binding. The lower bound is the costume it carries: the multi-chain-and-medallion scheme belongs to the likeness layer begun with the 1586 sitting. This dating overturns the older literature's assumption (Mortensen; Christianson) that the stamp copies de Gheyn's engraving — the engraving was cut only in autumn 1590, two years after the die was in use. The small die's own first anchor is much later: its documented carrier is the Calvin/Theocritus volume Tycho gave Hans Lindenov, registered at Uraniborg on 21 March 1597.A
Frederik II's funeral procession, 1588 — Tycho among the coffin-bearers5 June 15881589Frederik II died on 4 April 1588 and the procession to Roskilde took place on 5 June; the engraved suite is dated 1589.C
De Gheyn's first engraving (Westergaard 1377) — the 'overloaded' portraitautumn 15901 December 1590The tightest-dated object in the whole series, on Tycho's own letters: 'nuper Amsterodami cupro insculptae' — recently engraved at Amsterdam — he wrote on 1 December 1590, dispatching impressions to Thomas Savile, with further sendings to Rantzau (7 December) and Scultetus (8 December). The letters name no artist; the plate is signed by de Gheyn. The inscribed 'aetatis 40 … 1586' names the likeness's origin, not the plate's date.A
The Copenhagen pen drawing (SMK KKSgb5254) — the second engraving's production drawingafter 7 December 1590c. mid-1595The drawing is the working model for the second engraving — mirror-reversed against the print, needle-traced for transfer, the head at 1:1 with the engraved head (measured) — so it postdates the 7 December 1590 order for that engraving and predates the plate's completion, which Lambert Cornelisz's dated 1595 copy places by mid-1595. The 'ca. 1586?' sometimes attached to it is back-dating driven by the untenable Gemperlin attribution; the sheet's design content (the 1586 likeness of the 40-year-old) is older than the sheet. A residual alternative — one c. 1590 sheet serving both plates, v1's head also transferred from it — remains live and unexcluded: both engravings carry the identical AETATIS-40/1586 caption, and the documented v1→v2 manner shift (dense/tonal → lighter/linear) could make a single model read smoother in v1 and more furrowed in v2. The drawing's v2-matching age signature and worn cap are supporting but confounded observations (low–medium); the discriminating test — calibrated 1:1 scale registration of the head against NH 238 — has not been run.A
De Gheyn's second engraving (Westergaard 1394) — the corrected portrait7 December 1590 (ordered)mid-1595 (design attested in Tycho's hands via the Cornelisz copy); plate completion by end 1596 (Altena's outer bound)The lower bound is Tycho's own re-order in the complaint letter of 7 December 1590 ('aliam illic confieri curem' — I am having another made there). The upper bound (revised July 2026): Lambert Cornelisz's reversed copy of this portrait is dated 1595 in its plate and was in Tycho's hands by 5 September 1595 (his letter to Pontanus) — so the design existed by mid-1595. Whether Cornelisz copied the printed plate or the Vorlage drawing KKSgb5254 is contested (Mortensen 1946 p. 21: 'Forlæg: Pennetegningen'); the 1595 date therefore pins the design with high confidence and the plate's completion only probably — the hard ceiling for the finished plate is Altena's 'before the end of 1596'. This still retires the older account that dated the model-delivery to May 1596 (the painter in Tycho's diary on 4 May and Blaeu's departure on 24 May 1596 cannot explain a design that was already circulating in Cornelisz's copy the year before). The corrected heraldry gives an internal lower bound as well: the error-state was still circulating on the February–March 1592 prospectus, so the correction falls between 1592 and the plate's completion (by end 1596).A
Lambert Cornelisz's print (Westergaard 1409) — the copy Tycho disliked1595 (dated in the plate)5 September 1595 (in Tycho's hands)The plate carries its own date, 1595, and Tycho's letter to his former assistant Pontanus, written at Uraniborg on 5 September 1595, shows he had already received impressions — one had come earlier via Cort Aslaksson, who wrongly believed it engraved at Cologne. The letter is also the print's significance for the whole chronology: being a reversed copy of the second de Gheyn portrait, its date proves the v2 image existed in copyable form by mid-1595; it dates the finished v2 plate only on the leading (unproven) reading that Cornelisz copied a printed impression rather than the design — the diagnostic zones are unusable because Cornelisz replaced the background apparatus, the mirror evidence is mixed, and CERL records de Gheyn II as his teacher (undated), a possible access route to pre-plate material. Under the design-model reading the plate's own terminus loosens and the May-1596 Blaeu/Pictor channel reopens.A
The 1595 portrait medal — the only likeness in metal15951595The medal dates itself: the obverse legend reads 'EFFIGIES TYCHONIS BRAHE O F ... AETATIS 49' and the reverse carries the date 1595 beneath the Brahe arms — Tycho was 48 turning 49 that year. Whether it was struck in his lifetime is a separate and softer question, and the hedges have three distinct roots: Dreyer's 'it has been suggested' derives from — and truncates — Danske Magazin II (1746), pp. 166–168, whose editors offered an explicitly unprovable twin conjecture (the completion of the star catalogue and the great brass globe reaching usable form in 1595); Beckett's suggestion that Tycho gave the medal as a princely favour is independent of that, from a 1946 private letter to Mortensen, not derived from the 1746 discussion; and the 1746 editors also floated Tycho's nephew Tage Ottesen Tott, then 15–16, as a possible commissioner. No contemporary document of 1594–97 attesting the medal has been found in the material searched. So the object and its date are documented; the lifetime striking is a reasonable inference, not a documented fact.B
The aetatis-50 portrait (Skokloster / Edinburgh) — the exile likeness14 December 15961597The dating rests on two different texts, not one. The surviving Skokloster and Edinburgh canvases carry a third-person caption — 'ÆTATIS SVÆ ANNO 50 COMPLETO … QVO POST DIVTINVM IN PATRIA EXILIVM LIBERTATI DESIDERATÆ DIVINO PROVISV RESTITVTVS EST' — with no place and no year, verified by direct examination of high-resolution photographs of both canvases and by SHM's own catalogue transcription. The dated text is attested only on the lost Copenhagen picture, via Resen, Inscriptiones Hafnienses (1668), pp. 335–336: first person ('restitutus fui'), placed and dated VANDESBECHI AN. MDXCVII, and signed TYCHO BRAHE OT. That exemplar's year is settled at 1597 — Resen's apostrophus numeral; Dreyer 1890, p. 264, verified against the printed page image; TBOO IX.211, sourced in its own apparatus to Cod. Vindob. lat. 10686; DSL's '1598' is a transcription slip in the modern digital edition. The lower bound of 14 December 1596, the day Tycho completed his fiftieth year, follows from 'aetatis suae anno 50 completo' and stands; but the 1597 anchor transfers to the surviving canvases by type-identity inference only, since their own captions are undated.B
The painted portraits in the finest Mechanica copies — Tycho between Ptolemy and CopernicusJanuary 1598 (production of the Mechanica)24 October 1601The Mechanica was produced from January 1598, which sets the campaign's lower bound; Tycho's death on 24 October 1601 caps it, and the bulk of the presentation copies had gone out by about 1600. The Copenhagen copy's autograph dedication of 18 September 1600 is that exemplar's terminus ante quem. The former lower-bound basis — an 'Aet: LII' age inscription on the watercolours — is withdrawn: full-resolution inspection of the Copenhagen leaf (July 2026) found no age inscription of any kind, Mortensen's actual printed reading is 'Aet: LI', type-generic and anchored to Andersen's 1886 etching rather than to any leaf, and Vellev's 'LII' remains unlocated.B
The Týn church epitaph, Prague — the effigy that closes the series1604 (Kirstine's death — the only sourced terminus post quem for the erection; the 1601 burial dates the grave, not the monument)after 1604 (no sourced completion date)Tycho died in Prague on 24 October 1601 and was buried in the Týn church, but the monument's erection is sourced only as after his widow Kirstine's death in 1604 (Friis 1871 p. 298, Mortensen 1946 p. 74, Norlind 1970 p. 318); no source attests an earlier figure-stone phase. It therefore stands past the lifetime boundary — but it was made in Prague by people who had seen him, and it closes the series.B
The Kågeröd epitaph, 1613 — Tycho kneeling among his parents' children16131613The tablet is dated by its own erection, twelve years after Tycho's death.B
Tycho's portrait on the celestial globes — a distribution channel begun in his lifetime1594after 1630 (latest located specimen, Museo Galileo 348); terminal edition undeterminedThe practice begins with the van Langren globe of 1594 — the Tychonic (third) state of a 32.5-cm celestial line the family had issued since 1586 with pre-Tychonic positions (1586 Linköping; 1589 Greenwich GLB0099, positions probably Rudolf Snellius's; 1594 Frankfurt — Dekker, History of Cartography vol. 3, App. 6.1 nos. 130/137/148, verified verbatim, August 2026). Tycho's data and portrait were thus retrofitted onto an existing commercial product. Blaeu's own first celestial globe followed in 1597/98 (Dekker's census says 1597, Mosley p. 246 says 1598, citing the same van der Krogt pages; Warner 1971, the arbiter, unread) — it survives only as 9 of 12 gores at Houghton Library, Harvard (*51-2459 PF), and whether the portrait zone is among them is indeterminate. Earliest extant Blaeu specimen with confirmed portrait presence remains 1603. At the upper end, the latest securely located portrait-bearing specimen is the Joan Blaeu globe of after 1630; no source names a terminal portrait-bearing edition.A
The 1642 console clock — Tycho as proprietor of a world system15961642The plates' Latin traces to Tycho's own prints (the 1588 De mundi heading recast third-person; the motto printed 1596); 1627 is a lower bound only if the caption's unverified pictorial derivation holds, and is impossible if the competing unfooted 1623 date (Frombork inventory) is right. 1642 rests on the unfootnoted 2006 caption; the museum record is undated and no engraved date or signature is visible in the 2006 photograph.B
Heinrich Hansen, 1882 — Tycho as national history painting18821882Dated by the museum's catalogue record; corroborated by Eller 1978 (p. 43, 'Rekonstruktion af Heinrich Hansen 1882') and the 'H.H. 82' monogram reported on the sister version.A
Eckersberg's sketch, 1831 — the young Christian IV visiting Hven18311842The caption dates the related history painting to 1831 (diary: painting finished 23 March 1831; etchings May–June 1831; SMK KKS2591 inscribed May 1831). The sheet itself is undated: preparatory if the caption's 'til historiemaleri' framing is right (before March 1831), later if it belongs to the print campaigns, whose last documented drawing episode is 18 June 1842 (the block drawing for Flinch).C
The Levy memorial sheet — Tycho for childrenc. 18551901The sheet carries no date. The 2006 caption guesses 'måske 1901?' — with the question mark, which must be preserved; the guess is undefended in print, though it sits adjacent to the volume's ten pages of Prague-1901 material. Orthography and diction narrow nothing: 'ei' and 'smaae' are long-lived literary archaisms (ODS s.vv. 'ej', 'smaa'), and 'Uranienborg' is Tycho's own original name-form, used here as an antiquarian gesture. No publisher dates for any 'A. Levy' have been found in any consulted source, so the c. 1855 lower bound is conventional, not evidenced. The bounds remain wide for want of evidence.C
The full-figure watercolour at Strahov — the standing Tycho with a sextantc. 15981666The lower bound is the book it is bound into — a copy of the Progymnasmata that Tycho gave away, with his autograph dedication to Johann Zajíc von Hasenburk. But Friis warns that a drawing bound into a presented book need not be contemporary with the book: 'naar Vandfarvetegningen nu findes i en Bog, som Tyge Brahe har bortforæret, saa følger dog ikke heraf, at den er samtidig med Bogen.' The upper bound is Philip Kilian's engraving in Historia coelestis (Augsburg: Utzschneider, 1666, ed. Albert Curtz as 'Lucius Barrettus'), which the drawing either precedes as its model or follows as a derivative — Friis's 'Vienna 1657' imprint for the plate is refuted, and no documented 1657 issue exists. The 1881 report is now read at the source rather than through Friis: Vierteljahrsschrift der Astronomischen Gesellschaft 16, p. 273, in the Bayerische Staatsbibliothek's digitisation.C
Kilian's standing Tycho (1666) — a whole body invented around a borrowed head18 July 16581666The lower bound comes from the plate's companion. The same gathering carries a double-page frontispiece by the same hand, signed identically, showing four Habsburg emperors with putti holding banderoles — PARAVIT, …PAVIT, RECEPIT, PVBLICAVIT — a programme that culminates in the reigning emperor under whom the book was published, Leopold I. Leopold was not elected until 18 July 1658, so no plate in that gathering can predate it. The upper bound is publication: Historia Coelestis, Augsburg, Simon Utzschneider, 1666.A

6The descent of the likeness

The likeness of 1586 — the sitting behind the Frederiksborg oil and the woodcut — fed the print campaign. The first Amsterdam engraving of 1590 arrived overloaded with chains against his instructions; he ordered a corrected replacement within days, and that second engraving, produced from the surviving Copenhagen drawing and in copyable form by mid-1595, became the image Europe reproduced in his lifetime and after it. The gilt binding die ran on a separate track: in use by March 1588, carrying the loaded costume before the first engraving existed, and never re-cut. The exile portrait of 1597 and the Mechanica watercolours are fresh sittings, independent of the 1586 face.

The table gives every derivation the edition asserts, with its kind and its basis. Documented means a letter, a signed plate, a direct copy or a physical transfer mark settles it. Reconstructed means it is the leading reading where no document settles the path. Disputed means the direction of dependence is itself the open question. The figure draws the same edges on a time axis; it is a picture of the table, not a second source, and where the two could disagree the table governs.

The derivations 18: 11 documented, 5 reconstructed, 2 disputed

7Identification: the key, and the rejected identifications

How the likenesses are told apart, and how an identification is made or refused. The engraved family is the practical problem: a reader with a portrait in a book, a print in a cabinet or a painting on a wall needs to know which of the two de Gheyn states it derives from, and whether an inscribed date means anything. The discriminators below are the ones the records actually use; each is stated with the records it separates. The second part of the section keeps the identifications that have been proposed and rejected, with the ground of the rejection.

The discriminators

DiscriminatorReads one wayReads the otherCaution
The head: bare or in the plumed beretBare: the woodcut, the first engraving (beret on the parapet), the Frederiksborg oil, the Prague panel, the exile type, the Mechanica watercoloursBeret worn: the Copenhagen drawing, the second engraving, Cornelisz, the Gassendi frontispiece, Kilian, the 1594 globe miniature; the tapestry figure, if it is TychoThe beret is a return to how he dressed, not an invention of 1595, if the tapestry identification holds.
The Frederik II medal (F.S.) and the stacked chainsPresent: the portrait die, the first engraving, the Steensgaard panel (medal without Elephant)Absent: the second engraving and everything after it; the exile type and the watercolours carry other schemesThe die kept the loaded scheme for the rest of his life; a binding does not date by its costume.
The Order of the ElephantWorn: the Frederiksborg oil, the 1595 medal, the exile type; an elephant form on both dies and both engravingsAbsent: the mural effigy (no insignia at all), the tapestry figure, the Steensgaard panel, the Copenhagen watercolour (a plain knot)Christianson dates the grant to 3 May 1580, so the device gives a 1580 floor only; the usual ‘c. 1585/86’ is itself inferred from portraits, and dating portraits by it is circular. The 1746 eyewitness saw only ‘en Elefant’ on the die and named no order.
The ancestral quarteringsError state: BELKER where the Vasa sheaf belongs and HØNNOR for Rønnow — the woodcut and the first engravingCorrected: STORMVASE and RØNNOR — the second engraving and its copies (Gassendi)The woodcut has no corrected state; the error was still circulating on the 1592 prospectus.
The tablet’s vocabularyMachinarumque … dispositarum — the first engravingInstrumentorumque … dispositorum — the second engraving, Gassendi, KilianLegible on low-resolution digitisations, so it settles which engraving a given book carries.
The hands—Transposed left-for-right on the second engraving while the head is not mirrored: the fingerprint of a freshly drawn plate transferred from the Copenhagen drawingThe drawing is the mirror of the print with the head at 1:1; a copyist reproduces his source’s costume, and the drawing’s differs from both plates.
The inscribed ‘aetatis 40 … anno 1586’Carried by the woodcut, both engravings, the Gassendi frontispiece, the Weimar watercolour and Kilian’s standing figure of 1666It names the origin of the likeness and travels with the design. Wellcome’s catalogue dates Kilian’s plate 1586 from it.
The nose prosthesisShown: the Frederiksborg oil (reddened), the woodcut (as a plate on the bridge), the Týn effigy (plainly), the Copenhagen watercolour (per Biela, 1824)Absent from both de Gheyn states and from the exile type; Prandtl’s reading of it on Kilian’s plate is unconfirmedMortensen’s claim that the Prague panel indicates it is not visible on the only reproduction at any resolution.
Age and sense on the copiesCornelisz 1595: reversed, over two hemispheres, PRINCEPS in the ovalThe globe legends: ÆTATIS 47 computed fresh for 1594 and frozen on the 1613 Veen/Hondius globe; Blaeu’s miniature a reversed bustA frozen age on a later object points back to the model’s date, not the object’s.
Which engraving a book carriesEvery inserted portrait examined in Epistolae and Mechanica copies is the second engravingThe only physically verified book use of the first engraving is a 1602 Progymnasmata title versoInsertion was loose; copies with a watercolour substitute or no portrait at all are documented, and unexamined copies may differ.

Identifications considered and rejected 4

Things that have been called portraits of Tycho and are not, kept with the ground of each rejection so that a reader can judge whether the rejection is demonstrated or merely asserted. One of them is rejected on an argument its author never showed, and the edition records it as a rejection rather than as a demonstration.

The Næsbyholm painted ceiling — border medallions

rejectedrej-the-n-sbyholm-painted-ceiling-border-medallions
The claim
Implied to contain a portrait of Tycho by inclusion in a Tycho volume.
Verdict
Rejected. No source has ever proposed any head as Tycho.
Ground
Karl Madsen, Kunstens Historie i Danmark, pp. 62–64: 'Medaillonportræterne er sikkert for største Delen Portræter af Herremanden og hans Familie' — the squire being Steen Brahe, Tycho's brother. Absent from Mortensen, Dreyer, Friis, Norlind, Christianson, Beckett & Christensen and Studnička alike. Its presence in the 2006 book is a social-history illustration choice. The ceiling itself is at Nationalmuseet.

The Kunstkammer ivory box with portrait medallions

rejectedrej-the-kunstkammer-ivory-box-with-portrait-medallions
The claim
An 18th-century Kunstkammer tradition held that the two medallions showed Tycho Brahe and his wife.
Verdict
Rejected — but on an undefended argument.
Ground
Grinder-Hansen 2006: 'det hele er en skrøne' — the whole thing is a fable — because the man's head 'har ikke den fjerneste lighed med kendte portrætter af Tycho Brahe'. That is iconographic comparison without stated working: he names no compared portraits and shows no reasoning. The rejection is very probably right, and is recorded here as a rejection rather than as a demonstration. The object itself is untraced.

Langhans's photographs of the skull and upper body, Prague 1901

rejectedrej-langhans-s-photographs-of-the-skull-and-upper-body
The claim
Sometimes treated as images of Tycho.
Verdict
Out of scope, not false. These are documentary photographs of physical remains, not made likenesses.
Ground
Mortensen's census correctly ends at the Týn effigy, and the 2006 volume files them in a burial chapter, not a portrait chapter. Two things worth carrying: they falsify the claim, still circulating, that no photographic record survives from 1901; and the 'Det Kongelige Bibliotek' line under them is a reproduction credit, not a statement of ownership — no holding record for either photograph has been found.

'Tycho Brahe's chair' and the other Kunstkammer relics

rejectedrej-tycho-brahe-s-chair-and-the-other-kunstkammer-reli
The claim
Objects associated with Tycho by 18th-century tradition.
Verdict
Rejected. The chair is a Norwegian peasant chair.
Ground
Grinder-Hansen 2006, recording the Kunstkammer protocol of 1737 which enters a hollowed-trunk 'knubstol' as Tycho's chair from Uraniborg. Included here because the same appetite that produced the chair produced the ivory box: an 18th century that wanted Tycho relics and found them.

8Desiderata

Everything this edition does not know, in one place: the records without a reproduction, the objects whose location is attested rather than confirmed, the disagreements left open, the standing questions of the catalogue as a whole, and the questions each record carries — with, where the record names it, the single step that would close it. Several are cheap: a registry enquiry, a reading-room call slip, a caliper on a 1746 engraving.

Records without a reproduction 10

RecordWhy
The Stjerneborg astronomer cycle — Tycho as the terminus of his own scienceDestroyed. The superstructure was demolished or decayed in the earlier 17th century; Charlier excavated the foundations in 1901.
Frederik II's funeral procession, 1588 — Tycho among the coffin-bearersAn edition multiple. A complete 16-sheet set is at Kiel. The institution’s record is linked in the register.
The small gilt brass quadrant — an iconographic instrumentUntraced after the Hven era. The Prague-destruction account is collective (Dreyer, hedged) and item-level documentation is absent from the 1607–1650 Prague inventories; the instrument was obsolete by c.1580 and its presence in the 1600 Prague shipment is unconfirmed.
The posthumous copies (1602 onward)Dispersed edition multiples across European collections.
The Kågeröd epitaph, 1613 — Tycho kneeling among his parents' childrenIn situ in Kågeröds kyrka, Skåne. Placed in front of the blocked south portal (former vapenhus entrance) in the 1914 Wåhlin renovation; reportedly moved to the sacristy in the 2022 interior renovation (Regionmuseet Skåne KrM 2022:40 — title confirmed, report unread). The institution’s record is linked in the register.
Tycho's portrait on the celestial globes — a distribution channel begun in his lifetimeOne van Langren globe of the 1594 Tychonic state survives (Frankfurt); the 32.5-cm van Langren celestial line survives in three copies across its states (1586 Linköping, 1589 Greenwich, 1594 Frankfurt), the earlier two pre-Tychonic and portrait-less. Of Blaeu globes of 1603: Oxford (inv. 23709, portrait museum-confirmed) and Rome (Biblioteca Angelica); the Science Museum London specimen (1980-1913) is the same edition but its record is portrait-silent — catalogue silence being demonstrably non-diagnostic (RMG's granular record of its own 1603 Blaeu, GLB0101, likewise omits the portrait that edition carries). Later states after 1621 and after 1630 (the Metropolitan Museum's, portrait unconfirmed though printed from the 1603 plates; the Accorsi-Ometto's, asserted in a caption; the Museo Galileo's, confirmed). The institution’s record is linked in the register.
The 1642 console clock — Tycho as proprietor of a world systemOn display at the National Museum in Copenhagen, in the Renaissance galleries. The institution’s record is linked in the register.
Heinrich Hansen, 1882 — Tycho as national history paintingHanging at Frederiksborg, Room 27. The institution’s record is linked in the register.
Eckersberg's sketch, 1831 — the young Christian IV visiting HvenAttested at Frederiksborg on a single 2006 credit line (Fig. 2, p. 247). Characterised triple negative: the 2006 volume has no picture-credit apparatus (its back matter is a Litteraturliste only); absent from Frederiksborg's fully-enumerated published catalogue (c. 1,791 sitemap records) — which the museum itself states excludes its drawings holdings; absent from SMK (three etchings, zero drawings), from Weilbach, and from the demonstrably incomplete national aggregator (samlinger.slks.dk).
The Levy memorial sheet — Tycho for childrenAttested in Nationalmuseet's topographical archive on the museum's own 2006 statement; no live record found.

Objects whose location is attested, not confirmed 7

Each rests on a report never confirmed against a live institutional record, or on a last sighting at a stated date. The record names the enquiry that would close it.

Disagreements left open 28

Every conflict whose status is not marked resolved or settled, by record. The positions and the status are in the record.

Frederik II's funeral procession, 1588 — Tycho among the coffin-bearersThe title'Typus Pompae Funebris' is a genuine period title — BM P_1870-0514-2874 (1588 print) and BnF ark:/12148/cb32491664b (folio prepared by Rantzau, dedicated to Christian IV) — most plausibly of the 1588 funeral print series (per Lohmeier: 21 copperplates by Hogenberg alone, accompanying the letterpress Descriptio), distinct from the 1589 16-plate Res gestae in which the Kiel funeral plate is sheet 15. The open question is now which object Grinder-Hansen photographed.
